\title{The Era of 1-bit LLMs: All Large Language Models are in 1.58 Bits}

\begin{abstract}
Emerging developments such as BitNet~\cite{bitnet} are ushering in innovative directions for 1-bit Large Language Models (LLMs). In this study, we propose a ternary 1-bit LLM variant called \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, where each model parameter (weight) can take values from the set \{-1, 0, 1\}. This approach demonstrates that, for an equivalent model size and number of training tokens, our ternary model can achieve perplexity and downstream task results on par with its full-precision counterparts (FP16 or BF16 Transformers), yet offers remarkable reductions in latency, memory usage, throughput requirements, and energy demands. Significantly, this 1.58-bit LLM formulation establishes a novel scaling law and provides a new methodology for training future high-performing, cost-efficient LLMs. Additionally, it sets the stage for an alternative computational paradigm, enabling specialized hardware architectures optimized specifically for 1-bit LLMs.
\end{abstract}

\section{The Era of 1-bit LLMs}

Over the past few years, there has been substantial progress in the development and scalability of Large Language Models (LLMs) within the AI domain. While these models exhibit impressive abilities across numerous natural language processing applications, their ever-expanding scale introduces difficulties in practical implementation and intensifies worries about both ecological and financial costs arising from elevated energy requirements.

A common strategy for tackling these issues involves applying post-training quantization to produce low-bit models suitable for inference~\cite{smoothquant, gptq, quip, quip_sharp}. By lowering the bit-width of both weights and activations, this method substantially decreases the memory footprint and computational demands of LLMs. Recent developments have focused on transitioning from 16-bit representations down to more compact 4-bit formats~\cite{gptq, awq}. Nevertheless, despite its prevalence in production-level LLM deployments, post-training quantization remains a sub-optimal solution.

Recent advancements in 1-bit model designs, exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, indicate a valuable approach for lowering the computational expenses of LLMs without sacrificing effectiveness. Standard LLM implementations utilize 16-bit floating-point representations (such as FP16 or BF16), and matrix multiplication accounts for most of their computational workload. Consequently, the predominant source of computational overhead arises from floating-point arithmetic operations. In comparison, BitNet restricts its matrix multiplications to integer addition, resulting in substantial reductions in energy consumption for LLMs. Given that power consumption often sets the upper bound for computation on many hardware platforms, these energy reductions may also contribute to increased processing speed.

Beyond computation, moving model parameters from DRAM into the on-chip accelerator's memory, such as SRAM, can impose substantial overhead during inference. Efforts to increase the size of SRAM for enhanced throughput often result in costs that greatly exceed those of DRAM. 1-bit LLMs, relative to their full-precision counterparts, demand considerably less memory, both in terms of storage capacity and bandwidth. As a result, transferring weights from DRAM becomes less resource-intensive and quicker, enabling more rapid and cost-effective inference.

In this study, we present a notable 1-bit LLM variation termed \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, wherein each model parameter is ternary, assuming values from the set \{-1, 0, 1\}. By incorporating an additional 0 value into the standard 1-bit BitNet framework, the model operates at 1.58 bits within the binary numeral system. \text{BitNet b1.58} preserves all major strengths of its 1-bit predecessor, including its novel computation scheme that almost entirely eliminates multiplication in matrix operations, allowing for extensive optimization. It also maintains the same energy usage as the original 1-bit BitNet, while offering superior efficiency in memory, throughput, and latency relative to FP16 LLM baselines. Moreover, \text{BitNet b1.58} introduces two key improvements. First, it enhances representational power by explicitly supporting feature filtering—enabled by permitting weight values of 0—which considerably boosts the capabilities of 1-bit LLMs. Second, experimental results indicate that, beginning at the 3B parameter scale, \text{BitNet b1.58} is able to achieve parity with full-precision (i.e., FP16) baselines on both perplexity and downstream task metrics under comparable configurations (e.g., model size and training data volume).

\section{BitNet b1.58}

\text{BitNet b1.58} utilizes the BitNet architecture, a type of Transformer where the traditional \emph{nn.Linear} layer is substituted with \emph{BitLinear}. The model is initialized and trained from the beginning, employing weights quantized to 1.58 bits and activations at 8 bits. It departs from the original BitNet by incorporating several adjustments, outlined as follows.

\paragraph{Quantization Function.} To ensure weights are limited to the set \{-1, 0, +1\}, we employ the \emph{absmean} quantization function. This function initially normalizes the weight matrix by dividing by its mean absolute value, followed by rounding each entry to its nearest value in \{-1, 0, +1\}:

\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{W} = \mathrm{RoundClip}(\frac{W}{\gamma + \epsilon}, -1, 1),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{RoundClip}(x, a, b) = \max(a, \min(b, \mathrm{round}(x))),
\end{equation}

The quantization method applied to activations mirrors that utilized in BitNet, with the exception that, prior to the application of non-linear functions, activations are not rescaled to the $[0, Q_b]$ interval. Rather, for each token, activations are mapped to the range $[-Q_b, Q_b]$, thereby eliminating the necessity for zero-point quantization. This adjustment streamlines both the implementation and broader system optimization processes, and according to our experimental results, has only a minimal impact on performance.

\paragraph{LLaMA-alike Components.}

The LLaMA~\cite{llama, llama2} architecture has become the standard foundation for many open-source LLM initiatives. To align with and support this ecosystem, \text{BitNet b1.58} has been engineered with LLaMA-inspired structural elements. Notably, our model incorporates RMSNorm~\cite{rmsnorm}, SwiGLU~\cite{swiglu}, rotary embedding~\cite{rope}, and excludes all bias terms. This design choice ensures that \text{BitNet b1.58} can be readily deployed within major open-source platforms such as Huggingface, vLLM~\cite{vllm}, and llama.cpp\footnote{\url{https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp}}, requiring minimal adaptation.

\section{Results}

We conducted a comparative analysis between \text{BitNet b1.58} and our re-implemented FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} across multiple parameter scales. Both models underwent pre-training for 100 billion tokens using the RedPajama dataset~\cite{redpajama} to maintain experimental parity. For evaluation, we assessed zero-shot generalization on several language understanding benchmarks, including ARC-Easy~\cite{arc}, ARC-Challenge~\cite{arc}, Hellaswag~\cite{hellaswag}, Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, OpenbookQA~\cite{openbookqa}, and BoolQ~\cite{boolq}. Additionally, we measured validation perplexity using the WikiText2~\cite{wikitext2} and C4~\cite{c4} corpora.

We evaluated both the runtime GPU memory usage and inference latency for \text{LLaMA LLM} and \text{BitNet b1.58}. The measurements were obtained utilizing the FasterTransformer\footnote{\url{https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer}} framework, an inference engine tailored for low-latency LLM execution on GPUs. Additionally, for \text{BitNet b1.58}, we incorporated the Ladder~\cite{ladder} 2-bit kernel into our testing environment. To highlight the dominant expense in inference, we present the results in terms of time consumed per generated output token.

Table~\ref{tab:ppl} presents both the perplexity values and computational costs associated with \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. The results indicate that, in terms of perplexity, \text{BitNet b1.58} reaches parity with the full precision \text{LLaMA LLM} at a 3B parameter scale, yet achieves 2.71 times higher speed and utilizes 3.55 times less GPU memory. Notably, the 3.9B variant of \text{BitNet b1.58} is 2.4 times faster, requires 3.32 times less memory, and delivers considerably superior performance compared to the 3B version of \text{LLaMA LLM}.

Table~\ref{tab:zero-shot} presents a comprehensive breakdown of zero-shot accuracy across the evaluated end tasks. For this assessment, we adhered to the methodology outlined by \emph{lm-evaluation-harness}\footnote{\url{https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness}}. The data indicate that, with increased model size, the difference in performance between \text{BitNet b1.58} and the \text{LLaMA LLM} diminishes. Notably, from the 3B model size onwards, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves parity with the full-precision baseline. Echoing the trends observed in perplexity, the outcomes on end tasks highlight that \text{BitNet b1.58} at 3.9B surpasses \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B, all while requiring less memory and offering reduced latency. These results underscore \text{BitNet b1.58} as a Pareto improvement over current state-of-the-art LLMs.

\paragraph{Memory and Latency}

We extended our experiments by increasing the model sizes to 7B, 13B, and 70B, subsequently analyzing the associated costs. As shown in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy}, both latency and memory utilization exhibit clear scaling patterns, with acceleration gains becoming more pronounced as model size grows. Notably, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B demonstrates a 4.1-fold improvement in speed compared to the \text{LLaMA LLM} reference model. This improvement is attributed to the fact that the computational overhead of \emph{nn.Linear} increases with larger model sizes. Memory usage follows a comparable pattern because, although embeddings are maintained in full precision, their relative memory footprint diminishes as model scale increases. All measurements for latency and memory employed a 2-bit kernel, indicating that additional cost reductions are feasible with further optimization.

\paragraph{Energy}

We additionally analyze the energy requirements associated with arithmetic operations for both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. The analysis predominantly emphasizes matrix multiplication, as it represents the principal energy expenditure in large language models. Figure~\ref{fig:energy} displays the breakdown of energy consumption. Most of the energy consumed by \text{BitNet b1.58} originates from INT8 addition operations, whereas \text{LLaMA LLM} expends energy primarily on FP16 additions and multiplications. Referencing the energy estimation model from~\cite{energycost, pokebnn}, \text{BitNet b1.58} demonstrates a 71.4-fold reduction in arithmetic operations energy for matrix multiplications on 7nm technology compared to its counterpart. We extend this analysis by providing end-to-end energy consumption measurements for models processing 512 tokens. Our findings indicate that as model sizes increase, \text{BitNet b1.58} exhibits a greater relative advantage in energy efficiency over the FP16-based \text{LLaMA LLM}. This trend emerges because the proportion of energy devoted to \emph{nn.Linear} operations rises with larger models, while the contribution from other parts becomes less significant as model scale increases.

\paragraph{Throughput}

We evaluate the throughput performance of \text{BitNet b1.58} and the 70B-parameter \text{LLaMA LLM} across two 80GB A100 GPUs, employing pipeline parallelism~\cite{gpipe} to enable execution of the \text{LLaMA LLM} 70B model. By progressively increasing the batch size to the maximum capacity permitted by the GPU memory at a fixed sequence length of 512, we observed notable differences. As presented in Table~\ref{tab:throughput}, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B accommodates a batch size up to 11 times larger than that of \text{LLaMA LLM}, which translates to an 8.9-fold improvement in throughput.

\textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58} introduces a novel scaling relationship between model accuracy and inference resource demands}. By examining Figures~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} and \ref{fig:energy}, we observe the following correspondences for model size efficiency when comparing 1.58-bit and 16-bit representations:

\begin{itemize}
    \item The 13B BitNet b1.58 outperforms the 3B FP16 LLM regarding latency, memory footprint, and energy usage.
    \item The 30B BitNet b1.58 demonstrates greater efficiency than the 7B FP16 LLM across latency, memory requirements, and energy consumption.
    \item The 70B BitNet b1.58 surpasses the 13B FP16 LLM in latency, memory usage, and energy savings.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Training with 2T Tokens}

The quantity of training tokens plays a significant role in the effectiveness of LLMs. To assess how \text{BitNet b1.58} performs when scaled with respect to token count, we trained a \text{BitNet b1.58} model on 2T tokens, adhering to the data preparation strategy employed by StableLM-3B~\cite{StableLM-3B-4E1T}, which represents the current leading open-source 3B model. Evaluation for both models was conducted using a suite of benchmarks, including Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, SciQ~\cite{sciq}, LAMBADA~\cite{lambada}, and ARC-easy~\cite{arc}. The zero-shot accuracy outcomes are summarized in Table~\ref{tab:2t}. For tasks assessed with both accuracy and normalized accuracy metrics, we averaged the two scores. The results for StableLM 3B trained on 2T tokens are referenced from its official technical report. Our analysis demonstrates that \text{BitNet b1.58} consistently outperforms its counterpart across all evaluated tasks, suggesting that 1.58-bit LLMs are also highly capable of generalizing to diverse tasks.

\section{Discussion and Future Work}

\textbf{1-bit Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs}

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have demonstrated notable cost-efficiency for large language models (LLMs). Although MoE methods decrease computation in terms of FLOPs, their utility is constrained by substantial memory demands and considerable inter-chip communication requirements. The advent of 1.58-bit LLMs offers solutions to these obstacles. The decreased memory usage permits MoE models to be deployed across fewer devices. In addition, lower memory requirements translate to a marked reduction in the communication overhead for transmitting activations between nodes. Ideally, if the whole model fits within the memory of one chip, communication overhead is eliminated altogether.

\textbf{Native Support of Long Sequence in LLMs}

With the rise of LLMs, efficiently managing extended input sequences has emerged as a key requirement. A significant obstacle in processing lengthy sequences is the substantial memory usage caused by KV cache storage. \text{BitNet b1.58} makes considerable progress toward enabling native support for longer contexts by lowering activation precision from 16 bits to 8 bits. This reduction permits a doubling of context window length while maintaining identical resource constraints. Moreover, activations can potentially be compressed losslessly to 4 bits or below, particularly for 1.58-bit LLMs—a direction we propose for future investigation.

\textbf{LLMs on Edge and Mobile}

Deploying 1.58-bit LLMs presents a significant opportunity to boost the effectiveness of language models operating on edge and mobile platforms. Such platforms frequently encounter constraints in both memory and processing capacity, which can hamper the implementation and efficiency of traditional LLMs. With their notably lower demands for memory and power, 1.58-bit LLMs can be feasibly integrated into these environments, unlocking new possibilities for applications that were once infeasible. As a result, this advancement has the potential to substantially broaden the functionality of edge and mobile devices, fostering innovative uses of LLMs. In addition, 1.58-bit LLMs are especially compatible with CPUs—the primary type of processor in edge and mobile contexts—rendering it possible for models such as \text{BitNet b1.58} to operate with heightened efficiency and further expand the computational capabilities of these devices.

\textbf{New Hardware for 1-bit LLMs}

Recent developments such as~Groq\footnote{\url{https://groq.com/}} have shown encouraging outcomes and significant promise in constructing specialized hardware, such as LPUs, tailored for LLMs. Building on these advancements, we propose and advocate for the creation of novel hardware and system architectures that are explicitly fine-tuned for 1-bit LLMs, taking advantage of the innovative computation paradigm introduced in BitNet~\cite{bitnet}.

\end{document}<|im_end|>
------
United States Court of Appeals

FOR THE DISTRICT OF COLUMBIA CIRCUIT

Argued February 12, 2025 Decided October 3, 2025

No. 24-5168

AMERICA FIRST LEGAL FOUNDATION, APPELLANT

v.

JAMIESON GREER, IN HIS OFFICIAL CAPACITY, APPELLEE

Appeal from the United States District Court

for the District of Columbia (No. 1:22-cv-03576)

Andrew J. Block argued the cause for appellant. With him on the briefs was Reed D. Rubinstein.

David L. Peters, an attorney with the U.S. Department of Justice, presented oral argument on behalf of the appellee. Accompanying him on the briefs were Brian M. Boynton, who served as Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General when the brief was submitted; Brett A. Shumate, in the capacity of Acting Assistant Attorney General; and Matthew M. Graves, who held the position of United States Attorney at the time of filing. Michael

S. Raab, Attorney, Susan K. Ullman, General Counsel, U.S.

Office of Special Counsel, and Hnin Khaing, Senior Litigation Counsel.

GINSBURG, Senior Circuit Judge.

Opinion for the Court filed by Circuit Judge KATSAS.

KATSAS, Circuit Judge: America First Legal Foundation initiated this action seeking to require the Office of Special Counsel to examine if a federal agency unjustifiably rejected AFL’s request under the Freedom of Information Act. The district court ruled against AFL’s claims on substantive grounds. We determine, however, that AFL does not have Article III standing to advance these claims.

I

A

The Office of Special Counsel (OSC) functions as an investigative and prosecutorial body created to ensure “a fair, efficient, and lawfully-conducted Civil Service.” Frazier v. MSPB, 672 F.2d 150, 162 (D.C. Cir. 1982). Under the authority granted by the Civil Service Reform Act, the OSC is tasked with investigating “prohibited personnel practices.” If its investigation determines further action is necessary, OSC may initiate enforcement cases before the Merit Systems Protection Board to secure corrective measures pursuant to 5 U.S.C. § 1214 or pursue disciplinary sanctions in accordance with 5 U.S.C. § 1215. Section 1214 delineates the procedural framework for addressing prohibited personnel practices, which includes requirements for keeping complainants apprised of the investigation’s status. See, for instance, 5 U.S.C. § 1214(a)(1)(B)–(D).

Section 1216 grants OSC the authority to investigate and pursue remedies for specific additional categories of employee misconduct within the government. Pertinently, it mandates that OSC “shall … conduct an investigation of any allegation concerning arbitrary or capricious withholding of information prohibited under section 552”—that is, instances of information being withheld in violation of the Freedom of Information Act. U.S.C. § 1216(a)(3). When such alleged breaches of FOIA occur, OSC is authorized to “investigate and seek corrective action under section 1214 and disciplinary action under section 1215 in the same manner as if a prohibited personnel practice were implicated.” Id. § 1216(c).

FOIA includes a distinct section dedicated to OSC enforcement. According to this provision, when a court (1) directs the release of agency records that were wrongfully withheld under FOIA, (2) imposes attorneys’ fees on the agency, and (3) expressly raises concerns regarding whether the agency’s withholding was “arbitrary or capricious,” OSC is required to “promptly initiate a proceeding to determine whether disciplinary action is warranted against the officer or employee who was primarily responsible for the withholding.” U.S.C. § 552(a)(4)(F)(i).

B

In its inquiry into judicial recusals, AFL submitted a request to the Department of Justice seeking specific email correspondence exchanged between an external attorney and the DOJ’s Civil Division. AFL contends that the Civil Division rejected this request on questionable grounds, namely, that it refrains from searching employee email accounts for relevant documents without first obtaining the consent of those employees. Following this denial, AFL filed a lawsuit against DOJ pursuant to FOIA. In response to the litigation, DOJ has already provided—and continues to provide—documents that are responsive to AFL’s request. The adequacy of this ongoing production is not a matter for consideration in this context.

AFL further requested that OSC examine the DOJ’s purported FOIA practice of refusing to search employee emails without their permission. Citing section 1216(a)(3), which requires OSC to “conduct an investigation” into arbitrary FOIA denials, AFL emphasized this statutory duty. However, OSC declined to initiate an investigation, asserting that the prerequisites outlined in section 552(a)(4)(F)(i) necessary to commence such an inquiry had not been met in relation to the alleged FOIA policy. OSC also maintained that even though AFL referenced section 1216, it did not obligate OSC to investigate if the conditions prescribed by FOIA itself were lacking.

The district court partially ruled in AFL’s favor but ultimately dismissed the case on the grounds that it failed to state a viable claim. The court determined that section 552(a)(4)(F)(i) does not curtail OSC’s authority to pursue investigations of purported FOIA violations under section 1216. See Am. First Legal Found. v. Kerner, No. 22-cv-3576, 2023 WL 9546644, at *5 (D.D.C. Sept. 29, 2023). Accordingly, the court found that OSC’s refusal to investigate stemmed from an erroneous interpretation of the law. Id. However, the court declined to mandate that OSC investigate any FOIA-related policy, reasoning that section 1216(c) vests such discretion with OSC. Id. at *6. The matter was sent back to OSC for additional review. Id. When OSC again opted not to investigate, AFL requested entry of a final judgment, after which the district court dismissed the remainder of its claims. J.A. 68. AFL appealed, and we now conduct a de novo review. N. Am. Butterfly Ass’n v. Wolf, 977 F.3d 1244, 1249 (D.C. Cir. 2020).

II

Prior to addressing the substantive issues, it is necessary to determine if AFL met the prerequisites for Article III standing, as this issue is central to the district court’s subject-matter jurisdiction. See Steel Co. v. Citizens for a Better Env’t, 523 U.S. 83, 101–02 (1998). Since neither side originally discussed standing, we directed the parties to submit additional briefs specifically on this matter.

Article III of the Constitution confines the exercise of federal “judicial Power” to the resolution of particular “Cases” and “Controversies.” U.S. Const. Art. III, § 2, cl. 1. The doctrine of standing arises from this constitutional case-or-controversy limitation. Allen v. Wright, 468 U.S. 737, 750 (1984). For a plaintiff to meet the requirements of Article III standing, there must be a demonstration of an injury that is traceable to the conduct of the defendant and capable of being remedied by a favorable court ruling. Lujan v. Defs. of Wildlife, 504 U.S. 555, 560–61 (1992). Such injury must constitute “an invasion of a legally protected interest” and must be both “concrete and particularized” as well as “actual or imminent, not conjectural or hypothetical.” Id. at 560 (cleaned up). At the stage where a motion to dismiss is considered, the plaintiff is required to allege facts that plausibly establish each element necessary for standing. Food & Water Watch, Inc. v. Vilsack, 808 F.3d 905, 913 (D.C. Cir. 2015).

In its supplemental brief, AFL asserts that OSC infringed upon its purported procedural entitlement to an investigation as provided by section 1216. However, mere deprivation of a procedural right, absent any concrete interest adversely affected by such deprivation—a procedural right in isolation—does not satisfy the requirements for Article III standing. Summers v. Earth Island Inst., 555 U.S. 488, 496 (2009); see also Citizens for Const. Integrity v. Census Bureau, 115 F.4th 618, 625 (D.C. Cir. 2024). In an effort to address this deficiency, AFL argues that OSC’s decision not to pursue an investigation has resulted in two specific, tangible informational injuries: namely, that DOJ has failed to disclose the records requested by AFL under FOIA, and that OSC has withheld the notices and information mandated during the course of an investigation pursuant to section 1214.

By framing its claim in this manner, AFL attempts to take advantage of the more permissive standing standards applicable in cases asserting procedural injury. In these instances, when plaintiffs contend that a statutory procedural right—enacted to safeguard their specific, tangible interest—has been violated, courts tend to ease, but not eliminate entirely, the traditional requirements concerning imminence and redressability. However, courts maintain the requirements of injury in fact and causation. See Ctr. for Law & Educ. v. Dep’t of Educ., 396 F.3d 1152, 1157 (D.C. Cir. 2005); see also Fla. Audubon Soc’y v. Bentsen, 94 F.3d 658, 664–65 (D.C. Cir. 1996) (en banc). Plaintiffs in such procedural injury cases must still establish that the infringement of a procedural right has produced harm to a “concrete, particularized interest.” Ctr. for Law & Educ., 396 F.3d at 1157. Here, AFL fails to meet these obligations, including even the diminished threshold for redressability. Since AFL falls short of satisfying the procedural injury standing prerequisites, we need not resolve the distinction between procedural and substantive injuries in this context.

AFL’s primary theory is that OSC’s alleged neglect in initiating an investigation under section 1216 has ultimately resulted in AFL being deprived of the records it sought from DOJ through a FOIA request. This line of reasoning, however, is fundamentally flawed with respect to both causation and redressability. To begin with, any purported informational harm stemming from DOJ’s denial of access to agency documents is clearly attributable to DOJ, not to OSC. Furthermore, the hypothetical link between OSC’s inaction under section 1216 and DOJ’s decision not to fulfill the FOIA request is far too conjectural and indirect to fulfill the requirements for Article III standing. The speculative sequence postulated by AFL is as follows: Had OSC conducted the allegedly obligatory investigation pursuant to section 1216, OSC might have opted to initiate corrective action under section 1214, or disciplinary measures under section 1215, against the DOJ personnel implicated. See 5 U.S.C. §§ 1214(b)(3), 1215(a)(1). Subsequently, the MSPB could potentially have imposed sanctions on those employees for unjustified withholding of records under FOIA. See id. §§ 1214(b)(4)(A), 1215(a)(3)(A). As a result, DOJ might then have elected to release the requested documents. However, this hypothetical scenario rests on assumptions regarding the actions of two entities not party to the present litigation—the MSPB and DOJ—whereas “causation generally cannot rely on speculation about the unfettered choices made by independent actors not before the courts.” FDA v. All. for Hippocratic Med., 602 U.S. 367, 383 (2024) (internal quotation marks omitted). Additionally, it hinges on conjecture regarding how OSC might deploy its enforcement authority, a domain in which judicial intervention is generally prohibited. See Heckler v. Chaney, 470 U.S. 821, 831–32 (1985). In light of such uncertainty, AFL cannot demonstrate that a favorable outcome in this case “would very likely spur the next steps necessary to redress” the alleged informational harm. Hecate Energy LLC v. FERC, 126 F.4th 660, 667 (D.C. Cir. 2025). AFL also neglects the well-established principle that redressability is typically a function of whether a judgment is binding on the actual parties to the lawsuit, not on whether its rationale “might persuade actors who are not before the court.” Haaland v. Brackeen, 599 U.S. 255, 293–94 (2023). Therefore, AFL is unable to ground its standing on the asserted interest in the information it requested from DOJ under FOIA.

AFL’s alternative basis for standing, which rests on an alleged informational interest in the results of the OSC’s investigation, proves equally unpersuasive. For a plaintiff to establish informational standing, there must be a tangible harm stemming from the denial of information that a statute affirmatively grants the plaintiff the right to obtain. See Am. Soc’y for Prevention of Cruelty to Animals v. Feld Ent., Inc., 659 F.3d 13, 23 (D.C. Cir. 2011) (citing Zivotofsky ex rel. Ari Z. v. Sec’y of State, 444 F.3d 614, 618 (D.C. Cir. 2006)); see also FEC v. Akins, 524 U.S. 11, 21 (1998); Pub. Citizen v. DOJ, 491 U.S. 440, 448–50 (1989). In the present case, however, even assuming that section 1216(a)(3) imposes an obligation on OSC to investigate FOIA violations, the provision does not confer upon AFL a right to obtain information. AFL counters by arguing that, were OSC to undertake the mandatory investigation purportedly required by section 1216, it might at its discretion furnish AFL with notices and information analogous to those mandated during “prohibited personnel practice” investigations under section 1214. Nevertheless, AFL stops short of arguing that OSC is compelled to incorporate the procedural notice requirements of section 1214 into section 1216 proceedings. In fact, AFL concedes precedent establishing that OSC retains discretion with respect to applying section 1214’s procedural framework to section 1216 cases. See Carson v. U.S. Off. of Special Couns., 633 F.3d 487, 495–96 (6th Cir. 2011). Because there is no claim of a statutory right to information under section 1214, AFL cannot demonstrate a cognizable injury supporting its informational standing theory. For the same reasons, since OSC is not required to extend section 1214 notice procedures to section 1216 inquiries, a court order compelling a section 1216 investigation would not be likely to remedy the informational injury asserted by AFL.

III

Because AFL lacks Article III standing, we vacate the district court’s judgment and remand with instructions to dismiss for lack of subject-matter jurisdiction.

So ordered.

Acute neurological Behçet’s disease complicated by a syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone

Eiichi Kakehi, Yukako Matsumoto, Shigehisa Sakurai, Kazuhiko Kotani

Abstract:

Summary: We describe an uncommon presentation of acute neurological Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD) that was simultaneously identified with systemic Behçet’s disease (BD), fulfilling the criteria for definite BD, and further complicated by syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). A male patient in his sixth decade developed fever, headache, and various mucocutaneous manifestations, such as oral aphthae, folliculitis-like eruptions, genital ulcers, and was noted to have hyponatraemia. The clinical and mucocutaneous evidence supported a diagnosis of BD. Despite a normal brain MRI, analysis of cerebrospinal fluid revealed aseptic meningitis, in line with acute neuro-BD. Laboratory investigations established the presence of SIADH. Treatment with colchicine alone resulted in complete remission of mucocutaneous signs, neurological symptoms, and normalization of sodium levels, obviating the need for corticosteroids or additional immunosuppressive therapies. This report highlights a potential link between acute neuro-BD and SIADH, emphasizing the necessity for clinicians to be vigilant for simultaneous neurological and endocrine disorders in newly identified BD patients.

Background:

Behçet’s disease (BD) is a persistent, multisystem inflammatory condition, marked by recurring oral and genital ulcers, uveitis, as well as various skin manifestations. Neurological complications, identified as neuro-Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD), arise in about 10%–20% of individuals with BD and often appear several years subsequent to the initial diagnosis. Typically, acute neuro-BD manifests with meningoencephalitis and parenchymal brain lesions, which are most frequently located in the brainstem. Although neuro-BD tends to develop later, it is uncommon for both BD and neuro-BD to be diagnosed simultaneously. When simultaneous diagnosis occurs, clinicians must evaluate both mucocutaneous signs and neurological complaints, such as headaches, behavioral disturbances, psychiatric manifestations, and focal neurological deficits.

The syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) constitutes a common etiology of hyponatraemia and is frequently attributed to central nervous system (CNS) inflammation, malignancy, or use of specific medications. Owing to the inflammatory pathology characteristic of acute neuro-BD, these patients may face an increased risk of developing SIADH. While SIADH has been documented in association with BD patients undergoing therapies like cyclophosphamide, the occurrence of SIADH due solely to CNS inflammation in the context of acute neuro-BD, without any medication triggers, remains insufficiently established.

Here, we present an unusual instance in which BD and acute neuro-BD were identified at the same time, complicated by the onset of SIADH. This case underscores the need to recognize SIADH as a possible complication of acute neuro-BD, especially in patients exhibiting vague CNS symptoms and unexplained hyponatraemia.

Case presentation:

A man of Asian descent in his 60s arrived at the emergency department, reporting a 3-day duration of fever, headache, generalized fatigue, reduced appetite, ongoing hiccups, and joint pain localized to the shoulders and ankles. He also described experiencing oral aphthae.

His past medical history was notable for type 2 diabetes mellitus; however, he was not undergoing any medical management, largely due to a lack of awareness about his illness and failure to adhere to dietary or exercise recommendations. There was no significant family history, including no known instances of BD among his relatives. His alcohol intake was occasional, he was a non-smoker, and there were no recent episodes of travel, animal contact, or exposure to ill individuals. The patient was employed in the metalworking field and had not engaged in recent sexual activity.

During assessment, the patient was alert, though notably fatigued. Recorded vital parameters included a temperature of 38.1°C, blood pressure measuring 135/73 mm Hg, a regular heart rate of 119 beats per minute, respiratory rate at 19 breaths per minute, and oxygen saturation of 96% while breathing ambient air. Physical evaluation identified oral aphthae and rashes resembling folliculitis distributed on the face and trunk (figure 1A, B, and C). Examinations of the cardiopulmonary and abdominal systems were without significant findings. Manual muscle testing showed preserved strength in both upper and lower limbs. No peripheral oedema was present, and clinical assessment indicated a euvolemic state. The patient was subsequently hospitalized for additional diagnostic workup.

Investigations:

The initial laboratory analysis showed significant hyponatraemia at 124 mmol/L, alongside raised levels of creatine kinase (1349 U/L), aspartate aminotransferase (77 U/L), alanine aminotransferase (64 U/L), C-reactive protein (CRP) at 180 mg/L (reference, <3 mg/L), and erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 70 mm/hour (reference, <15 mm/hour), while renal function remained within normal limits. The patient also exhibited hyperglycaemia (plasma glucose 14.2 mmol/L) and an increased HbA1c of 8.2%, indicating inadequate glycaemic management. Screening for hepatitis B and C returned negative.

Endocrine assessment demonstrated thyroid-stimulating hormone within normal range (1.11 mIU/L; reference, 0.50–5.00 mIU/L), free T4 of 1.25 ng/dL (reference, 0.90–1.70 ng/dL), and a serum cortisol level of 42 µg/dL (reference, >6 µg/dL). Serum osmolality was decreased at 267 mOsm/kg (reference, <270 mOsm/kg), while urine osmolality was elevated (900 mOsm/kg; reference, >300 mOsm/kg), accompanied by a high urinary sodium level (88 mmol/L; reference, >20 mmol/L), suggesting compromised water excretion. Despite the presence of hypo-osmolar hyponatraemia, antidiuretic hormone (ADH) was found to be raised (22.4 pg/mL). Electrocardiogram results were normal, and imaging studies including chest X-ray and CT scans of the head, chest, and abdomen revealed no abnormalities. Collectively, these findings were indicative of SIADH.

By the third day, the patient's headache and vertigo intensified. Although neck rigidity and cranial nerve dysfunction were absent, central nervous system (CNS) inflammation was considered. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) of the brain did not reveal any notable abnormalities affecting the hypothalamus or other cerebral regions (figure 2). Analysis of the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) showed a slightly increased opening pressure (17 cmH₂O), elevated protein concentration (0.97 g/L), and a modest lymphocytic pleocytosis (11 cells/µL). Cultures and molecular diagnostics of the CSF for bacterial and mycobacterial organisms, as well as herpes simplex virus (HSV), varicella zoster virus (VZV), and cryptococcal antigen, returned negative results. Cytological assessment of the CSF demonstrated no evidence of malignant cells.

Additional assessments were undertaken to investigate possible systemic disorders. Workup for sarcoidosis indicated normal lysozyme levels (5.1 µg/mL; reference range 5–10) and angiotensin-converting enzyme levels (11.4 U/L; reference 7–25). Screening for hematological malignancy demonstrated an elevated soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) (704 U/mL; reference range, 122–496 U/mL), while testing for human T-cell leukaemia virus type 1 antibody was negative.

By the fifth day, the patient developed a painful ulceration of the scrotum (figure 1D). The concurrent presence of oral aphthous ulcers and folliculitis-like cutaneous lesions raised suspicion for a systemic presentation of BD. There was no pathergy reaction to skin pricking. Ocular evaluation showed no signs of uveitis. The patient tested positive for HLA-B51.

Differential diagnosis:

Upon initial assessment, the presence of fever, headache, general malaise, and hyponatraemia pointed toward either a central nervous system infection or a systemic inflammatory process. Viral meningoencephalitis, particularly due to HSV or VZV, was the primary concern. Nonetheless, cerebrospinal fluid analysis returned negative for viral antigens and antibodies, thereby eliminating this possibility. Bacterial meningitis was similarly contemplated but dismissed owing to a lack of neutrophilic dominance, only a mild lymphocytic pleocytosis, and sterile CSF cultures. Tuberculous meningitis was ruled out as acid-fast staining and tuberculosis DNA tests were negative.

Given the neurological symptoms, a primary CNS malignancy was another consideration. However, imaging via brain MRI revealed no abnormalities, and no malignant cells were identified in CSF cytology. Although the soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) level was modestly elevated, this by itself was insufficient to confirm CNS lymphoma.

Autoimmune disorders were systematically excluded on the basis of absent hallmark systemic manifestations and negative autoantibody screening tests. Neurosarcoidosis remained in the differential diagnosis as normal MRI findings can occur in its early stages, but further laboratory workup ultimately argued against it. The slight increase in sIL-2R was deemed non-specific.

The diagnosis of definite BD was established according to clinical presentation: fever and fatigue, oral aphthous ulcers (assigned two points), genital aphthosis (two points), and a folliculitis-like eruption (one point). He accumulated five points based on the International Criteria for Behçet’s Disease (with a threshold of four or more for a definitive diagnosis). Notably, the presence of genital aphthosis is highly specific for BD and was a strong indicator in this context. After thoroughly excluding infectious, neoplastic, and autoimmune etiologies, the diagnosis of acute neuro-BD was confirmed, characterized by CNS involvement in the presence of mucocutaneous manifestations.

Treatment:

Upon admission (day 1), clinicians suspected the presence of an underlying inflammatory disorder, though its precise cause was not identified. Empirical cefepime therapy commenced after obtaining blood cultures, in order to address a possible bacterial infection, despite the lack of an identifiable source.

By day 3, the patient’s headache and vertigo had intensified, raising concerns about possible CNS inflammation or infection. Empirical acyclovir therapy was started, but discontinued once laboratory results excluded HSV and VZV encephalitis.

On day 8, following the confirmed diagnosis of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, colchicine therapy was introduced. Oral aphthous ulcers received topical corticosteroid ointment, while genital ulcers and skin lesions were managed with silver sulfadiazine cream. For SIADH, management consisted of fluid restriction to less than $800\,\mathrm{mL}/\mathrm{day}$, with vigilant daily serum sodium monitoring and caution to prevent overly rapid sodium correction. Treatment with hypertonic saline or vasopressin receptor antagonists was not necessary.

Outcome and follow-up:

The patient experienced resolution of his fever and an increase in appetite within a day of initiating colchicine therapy. Over the subsequent two weeks, there was gradual improvement in his symptoms, such as vertigo, general malaise, hiccups, and mucocutaneous manifestations. By the sixteenth day, his serum sodium concentration had returned to 136 mmol/L and ADH levels had dropped to 1.8 pg/mL. Additionally, levels of myogenic enzymes normalized.

Notably, the management plan did not include systemic corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive agents. The patient was discharged after spending one month in the hospital, a period during which the acute phase resolved, hyponatraemia was actively monitored and treated, and rehabilitation addressed his decreased physical capabilities. Colchicine was maintained for roughly four years, during which neither BD relapse nor neurological complications were observed. Upon stabilization of BD, biguanide therapy was started, resulting in sustained glycaemic control.

Discussion:

In this instance, both systemic BD and acute neuro-BD were concurrently identified, representing an unusual clinical occurrence. The case also illustrated that acute neuro-BD can be accompanied by SIADH. The improvement of mucocutaneous findings, neurological symptoms, and hyponatraemia following colchicine monotherapy indicates a potential direct link among BD, neuro-BD, and SIADH. When encountering patients with BD who develop fever or headache together with hyponatraemia, healthcare providers should entertain the possibility of acute neuro-BD as an underlying etiology.

The simultaneous detection of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD in this case, substantiated by the presence of mucocutaneous lesions and central nervous system involvement, is particularly noteworthy. Ordinarily, manifestations in major organs, such as CNS lesions, emerge on average about four years after a BD diagnosis, and neurological signs are initially present in fewer than 5% of individuals. Additionally, only around 10% of patients develop headaches attributable directly to neuro-BD, rendering early identification of neuro-BD particularly difficult in this context. Most frequently, acute neuro-BD manifests as a self-limiting meningoencephalitis, which may or may not be accompanied by focal neurological deficits. In this patient, MRI of the brain did not reveal any abnormalities at typical BD-affected regions, such as the hypothalamus. Acute neuro-BD is capable of presenting as aseptic meningitis in the absence of parenchymal brain involvement, and a normal brain MRI, especially during the initial stage, does not preclude the diagnosis. The absence of radiological evidence contributed to the diagnostic challenge in this scenario. Although testing for HLA-B51 was undertaken, it did not assist in diagnosis. This is consistent with international criteria for BD, which prioritize clinical findings and do not mandate HLA-B51 positivity. Ultimately, this case underscores the necessity of considering neuro-BD when patients exhibiting mucocutaneous symptoms also report non-specific neurological complaints.

During the initial steps of the differential diagnosis, our main consideration was an infectious etiology for the CNS inflammation, given the presence of unexplained fever, neurological manifestations, and hyponatraemia; nonetheless, no causative pathogen was identified. We expanded our differential to encompass other potential sources of CNS inflammation such as connective tissue disorders and neoplastic processes, specifically including sarcoidosis and haematologic malignancies. Consequently, our comprehensive preliminary workup incorporated autoimmune serological markers, as well as serum ACE and sIL-2R levels, as general screening measures. These investigations were undertaken largely to rule out possibilities rather than out of significant clinical suspicion. Ultimately, neither clinical evaluation, imaging, nor CSF analysis substantiated any of these conditions, and the ancillary tests did not significantly influence our diagnostic conclusions. The sensitivity of ACE for sarcoidosis remains suboptimal, and although sIL-2R might have utility in assessing malignant lymphoma, slight elevations lack specificity and can simply indicate a generic inflammatory response. In this instance, the absence of genital ulcers early in the disease course contributed to uncertainty regarding a diagnosis of BD and prompted an extensive differential approach. This case underscores the necessity of judicious test selection, reminding clinicians to reserve ancillary investigations for situations where they are clearly indicated by clinical context, and to interpret such results with discernment.

While drug-induced SIADH has previously been documented in individuals with BD treated with agents such as cyclophosphamide, the link between acute neuro-BD and the onset of SIADH is not well elucidated. Neuro-BD is defined by inflammatory processes within the CNS, which can potentially result in abnormal secretion of ADH. Evidence from multiple investigations has indicated that interleukin-6 (IL-6)—a proinflammatory cytokine commonly heightened in neuroinflammatory states—is implicated in the enhancement of ADH secretion. In the context of BD, dysregulated monocyte activity results in autonomous IL-6 generation, with increased IL-6 concentrations observed in the CSF of those experiencing acute neuro-BD episodes. This body of research has led to the hypothesis that IL-6–driven inflammation might be involved in the emergence of SIADH in this context. Despite the absence of direct IL-6 measurements in serum or CSF in the current report, the significantly raised serum CRP could indicate an underlying inflammatory process mediated by IL-6. SIADH is recognized as a complication of numerous conditions, encompassing CNS inflammation, pulmonary disorders, neoplasms, and select pharmacotherapies; however, the data presented here are insufficient to conclusively demonstrate a direct causative relationship between SIADH and acute neuro-BD. The temporal concurrence seen in this instance might simply represent a general reaction to CNS inflammation rather than indicating a mechanism specific to this disease entity.

This case further highlights the range of complications that may arise from hyponatraemia induced by SIADH, particularly in the context of acute neuro-BD. Prior literature has suggested that persistent hiccups could serve as a valuable clinical indicator of severe hyponatraemia stemming from SIADH associated with central nervous system inflammation, such as meningitis. Moreover, our initial diagnostic approach included assessing CK levels due to symptoms of fever, arthralgia, and generalized fatigue—findings that may be linked to inflammatory or metabolic muscle injury—and this testing uncovered rhabdomyolysis. Although rhabdomyolysis is a recognized complication of severe hyponatraemia, it is frequently missed in standard evaluations. These clinical presentations, while established, are frequently underestimated manifestations of marked hyponatraemia. It is important for healthcare providers to recognize that symptoms such as persistent hiccups or rhabdomyolysis, which may initially appear unrelated, could signal significant electrolyte imbalances, especially against a background of neuroinflammatory disease.
------
Corticosteroids remain the cornerstone therapy for acute neuro-BD, with the majority of patients showing a positive clinical response. Nonetheless, there are documented instances where patients exhibiting only meningoencephalitis-like features experience spontaneous remission. In the case discussed here, administration of colchicine alone resulted in improvements in both mucocutaneous and neurological manifestations, as well as correction of hyponatraemia. Colchicine mediates its anti-inflammatory activity through inhibition of neutrophil chemotaxis and activation, along with downregulation of pro-inflammatory cytokines such as IL-1β and IL-6. While colchicine is routinely employed for managing mucocutaneous symptoms in BD, meta-analyses indicate limited efficacy for acute neuro-BD, underscoring the need for immunosuppressants to achieve sufficient disease control. Previously, colchicine has mainly been administered in conjunction with corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive medications, rather than as sole therapy. Even though spontaneous improvement cannot be definitively ruled out in this instance, these observations imply that colchicine monotherapy may represent a viable therapeutic alternative for acute neuro-BD and associated complications like SIADH, especially in situations where corticosteroids are not recommended or are contraindicated.

Conclusion:

We presented an uncommon instance in which Behçet’s Disease (BD) and acute neuro-BD were diagnosed simultaneously, and where SIADH served as a complicating factor for the neuro-BD. This report underscores that SIADH can manifest as a consequence of acute neuro-BD, emphasizing the necessity for clinicians to recognize this potential link. Administration of colchicine alone resulted in swift resolution of mucocutaneous lesions, neurological deficits, and hyponatraemia, indicating that colchicine could be an effective initial treatment in early neuro-BD. For individuals with BD who develop vague central nervous system complaints such as fever and headache alongside hyponatraemia, it is imperative for healthcare providers to consider the possibility of acute neuro-BD complicated by SIADH.<|im_end|><|im_end|> did not spend your money wisely."

\title{The Era of 1-bit LLMs: All Large Language Models are in 1.58 Bits}

\begin{abstract}
Emerging work, exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, heralds the advent of 1-bit Large Language Models (LLMs). Here, we propose a new 1-bit LLM variant, \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, whose parameters are exclusively drawn from the ternary set \{-1, 0, 1\}. Remarkably, this approach achieves parity with full-precision Transformer LLMs (FP16 or BF16) of equivalent architecture and training scale, both in perplexity and downstream task efficacy. Moreover, this model offers substantial improvements in latency, memory usage, throughput, and energy efficiency. Beyond its efficiency gains, the 1.58-bit LLM introduces a novel scaling law and establishes a new methodology for training future LLMs that are simultaneously performant and resource-friendly. This innovation not only fosters a shift in computational paradigms but also creates new opportunities for hardware explicitly optimized for 1-bit LLMs.
\end{abstract}

\section{The Era of 1-bit LLMs}

The development of Large Language Models (LLMs) has accelerated dramatically, enabling unprecedented capabilities in natural language understanding and generation. Nonetheless, as model sizes have grown, practical concerns around deployment have intensified, particularly regarding their escalating energy demands and resultant environmental as well as economic costs.

A prominent strategy to mitigate these issues is the application of post-training quantization methods, which aim to create compact, low-bitwidth models optimized for inference~\cite{smoothquant, gptq, quip, quip_sharp}. These approaches operate by lowering the precision of both weights and activations, yielding significant savings in memory footprint and computation. There has been a continual movement from standard 16-bit formats toward even smaller representations, such as 4-bit models~\cite{gptq, awq}. However, while such quantization techniques are commonplace in industry applications of LLMs, they often result in less-than-ideal performance due to inherent limitations post-training.

Advancements in 1-bit model designs, as exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, indicate a promising avenue for reducing the computational demands of large language models (LLMs) without compromising on their efficacy. Conventional LLMs generally utilize 16-bit floating-point representations (such as FP16 or BF16), and most of their computational workload centers around matrix multiplications. As a result, the predominant source of computational expense stems from floating-point additions and multiplications. By contrast, BitNet confines its matrix multiplication operations to integer additions alone, achieving significant reductions in energy consumption for LLM inference. Since power is a primary constraint on computational throughput in many hardware architectures, these energy savings can be translated directly into increased computational speed.

Beyond just computation, the expense of moving model parameters from off-chip DRAM into on-chip memory resources (such as SRAM) is non-trivial during inference. Solutions have been proposed that scale up on-chip SRAM to enhance throughput, but this substantially increases system cost relative to DRAM. In contrast, 1-bit LLMs offer marked reductions in both memory bandwidth and storage requirements, alleviating bottlenecks associated with parameter loading from DRAM and improving inference speed and efficiency.

This paper presents \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, an innovative 1-bit LLM variant characterized by ternary parameters, each adopting one of \{-1, 0, 1\}. By extending the original BitNet’s binary representation with an explicit zero value, we arrive at an effective 1.58 bits per parameter in the binary system. \text{BitNet b1.58} preserves the advantageous computation scheme of BitNet—requiring minimal multiplication in matrix operations and remaining highly amenable to optimization. It matches the original BitNet’s energy profile while outperforming traditional FP16 LLMs regarding memory usage, data throughput, and latency. Importantly, \text{BitNet b1.58} introduces two distinct improvements: (1) the enhanced modeling capability enabled by ternary weights—specifically, the use of zero values allows for explicit feature selection and pruning, which notably boosts the representational power of 1-bit LLMs; and (2) empirical results demonstrate that \text{BitNet b1.58} can reach parity with full-precision baselines (FP16) concerning both perplexity and downstream task metrics, given identical settings (model size, number of training tokens, etc.) for models starting at the 3B parameter scale.

\section{BitNet b1.58}

\text{BitNet b1.58} extends the BitNet model, a Transformer variant that swaps the standard \emph{nn.Linear} layers for \emph{BitLinear} alternatives. This version is trained anew using weights quantized to 1.58 bits and activations quantized to 8 bits. In relation to the original BitNet, this model includes several modifications, which we outline below.

\paragraph{Quantization Function.} In order to limit the weights to values in $\{-1, 0, +1\}$, we utilize a quantization approach based on the \emph{absmean}. This method involves normalizing the weight matrix by dividing by the mean of the absolute values, followed by rounding each element to the nearest integer among $\{-1, 0, +1\}$:

\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{W} = \mathrm{RoundClip}(\frac{W}{\gamma + \epsilon}, -1, 1),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{RoundClip}(x, a, b) = \max(a, \min(b, \mathrm{round}(x))),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \gamma = \frac{1}{nm}\sum_{ij} |W_{ij}|.
\end{equation}

The same quantization method as used in BitNet is applied to the activations, with one key adjustment: rather than scaling pre-nonlinearity activations to fit $[0, Q_b]$, we rescale activations per token to the interval $[-Q_b, Q_b]$, effectively removing zero-point quantization. This adjustment streamlines both code implementation and system optimization, with our experiments showing minimal influence on performance.

\paragraph{LLaMA-alike Components.}

Given that the LLaMA~\cite{llama, llama2} architecture has become a standard foundation in the open-source LLM ecosystem, \text{BitNet b1.58} has been designed to closely mirror LLaMA's structure. This includes adopting RMSNorm~\cite{rmsnorm}, SwiGLU~\cite{swiglu}, and rotary embeddings~\cite{rope}, as well as omitting all bias terms. As a result, \text{BitNet b1.58} can be readily deployed within widely-used open-source frameworks, such as Huggingface, vLLM~\cite{vllm}, and llama.cpp\footnote{\url{https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp}}, requiring only minor modifications.

\section{Results}

We conducted a comparison between \text{BitNet b1.58} and our implementation of the FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} across multiple model scales. For consistency, both models underwent pre-training on the RedPajama dataset~\cite{redpajama} using 100 billion tokens. We assessed zero-shot capabilities on a suite of language benchmarks, namely ARC-Easy~\cite{arc}, ARC-Challenge~\cite{arc}, Hellaswag~\cite{hellaswag}, Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, OpenbookQA~\cite{openbookqa}, and BoolQ~\cite{boolq}. Validation perplexity was also recorded on WikiText2~\cite{wikitext2} and C4~\cite{c4}.

In terms of operational metrics, we evaluated GPU memory footprint and inference latency for both \text{LLaMA LLM} and \text{BitNet b1.58}. Measurements were obtained utilizing the FasterTransformer\footnote{\url{https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer}} library, which is optimized for minimizing latency during LLM inference on GPUs. Notably, the 2-bit kernel from Ladder~\cite{ladder} was incorporated for \text{BitNet b1.58}. For inference efficiency, we reported latency as the time required per generated token.

Results are summarized in Table~\ref{tab:ppl}, detailing both perplexity and computational costs for \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. The table reveals that, starting at a 3B model size, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves comparable perplexity to the full-precision \text{LLaMA LLM}, while delivering a 2.71$\times$ reduction in latency and a 3.55$\times$ decrease in GPU memory consumption. In particular, the 3.9B variant of \text{BitNet b1.58} is 2.4$\times$ faster and uses 3.32$\times$ less memory, yet substantially outperforms the \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B model.

Comprehensive zero-shot accuracy for all downstream tasks is presented in Table~\ref{tab:zero-shot}. We employed the protocol established by \emph{lm-evaluation-harness}\footnote{\url{https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness}} for these evaluations. The findings indicate that, as model size grows, the accuracy gap between \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} diminishes. Significantly, \text{BitNet b1.58} matches full-precision performance beginning from the 3B size. Consistent with perplexity results, on downstream tasks, \text{BitNet b1.58} 3.9B outperforms \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B, all while utilizing less memory and incurring lower latency. This evidence positions \text{BitNet b1.58} as a Pareto optimal enhancement over leading large language models.

\paragraph{Memory and Latency}

We expanded our experiments to include larger models at 7B, 13B, and 70B parameters, analyzing their associated costs. As shown in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy}, latency and memory usage trends reveal that acceleration gains improve with increased model scale. Notably, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B achieves a speed-up factor of 4.1 over the \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline, attributed to the fact that \emph{nn.Linear} computation time escalates as models grow. Memory usage displays a parallel pattern, since the embedding layer—remaining in full precision—accounts for a relatively smaller fraction of total memory in larger networks. Both performance metrics were gathered using a 2-bit kernel, implying that further reductions in cost are attainable through additional kernel optimization.

\paragraph{Energy}

To assess energy efficiency, we estimated the arithmetic operation energy consumption of both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. Our analysis prioritizes matrix multiplication, given its dominant contribution to the computational cost in large language models. Figure~\ref{fig:energy} depicts the breakdown of energy expenditure: most of the computation for \text{BitNet b1.58} is performed using INT8 addition, whereas \text{LLaMA LLM} primarily relies on FP16 addition and FP16 multiplication. Drawing on the energy model presented in~\cite{energycost, pokebnn}, we determined that \text{BitNet b1.58} reduces matrix multiplication energy consumption for arithmetic operations by a factor of 71.4 on 7nm hardware. We also report end-to-end energy usage for processing 512-token sequences. The results indicate that as model size increases, \text{BitNet b1.58} demonstrates progressively greater energy efficiency relative to the FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline. This improvement arises because the relative share of \emph{nn.Linear} operations grows with model scale, while the proportional costs of other components decline in larger models.

\paragraph{Throughput}

We assess the throughput of both \text{BitNet b1.58} and the 70B-parameter \text{LLaMA LLM} models using two 80GB A100 GPUs, leveraging pipeline parallelism~\cite{gpipe} to enable the execution of \text{LLaMA LLM} 70B on these devices. By incrementally increasing the batch size until the GPUs’ memory capacity was reached, while maintaining a sequence length of 512, we observed that \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B sustains a batch size up to 11 times larger than that of \text{LLaMA LLM}. This leads to an 8.9-fold increase in throughput, as presented in Table~\ref{tab:throughput}.

\textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58} introduces a novel scaling dynamic between inference cost and model effectiveness.} Drawing on Figures~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} and \ref{fig:energy}, we can express the following comparative relations for various model sizes using 1.58-bit versus 16-bit precision:

\begin{itemize}
    \item A 13B \text{BitNet b1.58} model surpasses a 3B FP16 LLM regarding latency, memory efficiency, and energy utilization.
    \item A 30B \text{BitNet b1.58} model outperforms a 7B FP16 LLM on latency, memory use, and power consumption.
    \item A 70B \text{BitNet b1.58} model is more advantageous than a 13B FP16 LLM with respect to latency, memory demands, and energy usage.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Training with 2T Tokens}

Training token count plays a vital role in large language models. To evaluate the scalability of \text{BitNet b1.58} with respect to token volume, we trained a \text{BitNet b1.58} model using 2T tokens, adhering to the dataset preparation methods outlined for StableLM-3B~\cite{StableLM-3B-4E1T}, which is recognized as the leading open-source model of its scale. Both models underwent evaluation on a composite benchmark comprising Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, SciQ~\cite{sciq}, LAMBADA~\cite{lambada}, and ARC-easy~\cite{arc}. Zero-shot accuracy metrics are reported in Table~\ref{tab:2t}; for evaluations involving both accuracy and normalized accuracy, we report the mean value. StableLM 3b's 2T token performance metrics are sourced from its technical documentation. Our analysis demonstrates that \text{BitNet b1.58} consistently outperforms across all evaluated tasks, implying robust generalization for 1.58-bit LLMs.

\section{Discussion and Future Work}

\textbf{1-bit Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs}

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have emerged as a resource-efficient strategy for large language models (LLMs), substantially lowering computational FLOPs. However, the practical deployment of MoE models remains hampered by substantial memory demands and significant inter-device communication costs. The introduction of 1.58-bit LLMs provides viable solutions to these limitations. Specifically, their reduced memory requirements decrease the number of devices necessary for deployment and dramatically lessen network activation transfer overhead. Ideally, with sufficient memory efficiency, entire MoE models might reside on a single chip, eliminating communication overheads altogether.

\textbf{Native Support of Long Sequence in LLMs}

Handling extensive input sequences has become increasingly crucial in LLM applications. One prominent issue is the elevated memory usage arising from key-value (KV) caches during inference on long sequences. \text{BitNet b1.58} addresses this concern by reducing activation precision from 16 bits to 8 bits, thereby enabling a twofold increase in context length with identical hardware constraints. Prospects exist for further, lossless compression—down to 4 bits or even less for 1.58-bit LLMs—suggesting promising avenues for subsequent investigation.

\textbf{LLMs on Edge and Mobile}

Deploying 1.58-bit LLMs can greatly enhance the feasibility and efficiency of language models on edge and mobile platforms, which commonly face strict limitations in terms of memory capacity and computational resources. The minimized memory and energy consumption characteristics of 1.58-bit LLMs enable their integration into such resource-constrained environments, broadening the spectrum of possible applications beyond previous constraints. As a result, these advancements could markedly elevate the functionality of edge and mobile devices. In addition, the architecture of 1.58-bit LLMs is well-suited to CPU-based systems—the predominant processing units in these devices—allowing \text{BitNet b1.58} to achieve higher operational efficiency and expanded capabilities on such platforms.

\textbf{New Hardware for 1-bit LLMs}

Innovative hardware solutions such as~Groq\footnote{\url{https://groq.com/}} illustrate the promise of developing dedicated platforms (e.g., LPUs) for LLM acceleration. Expanding on these efforts, we advocate for focused research and development targeting new hardware and system designs tailored specifically for 1-bit LLMs, motivated by the paradigm shift introduced by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}.<|im_end|>
