Acute neurological Behçet’s disease complicated by a syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone

Eiichi Kakehi, Yukako Matsumoto, Shigehisa Sakurai, Kazuhiko Kotani

Abstract:

Summary: This paper presents an unusual instance of acute neurological Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD) identified simultaneously with systemic Behçet’s disease (BD), fulfilling the criteria for definite BD, and further complicated by syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). A male patient in his sixties arrived with symptoms of fever, headache, and mucocutaneous lesions—namely, oral aphthae, folliculitis-like eruptions, genital ulcers, and hyponatraemia. Diagnosis of BD was established on the basis of his mucocutaneous findings. While brain MRI yielded no pathological findings, analysis of the cerebrospinal fluid indicated aseptic meningitis, supporting acute neuro-BD. Additional laboratory results met criteria for SIADH. Treatment with colchicine alone resulted in the complete remission of the patient’s mucocutaneous and neurological symptoms, as well as normalization of sodium levels, without resorting to corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive therapies. This report highlights a potential link between acute neuro-BD and SIADH, emphasizing the necessity to consider both neurological and endocrine complications at the time of initial BD diagnosis.

Background:

Behçet’s disease (BD) is a persistent, multisystemic inflammatory condition marked by episodic oral and genital ulcerations, uveitis, and cutaneous manifestations. Neurological involvement—termed neuro-Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD)—arises in roughly 10%–20% of cases, generally manifesting years after the initial identification of BD. Acute neuro-BD most frequently manifests as meningoencephalitis accompanied by parenchymal brain abnormalities, with a predilection for the brainstem. While it is more usual for neurological features to develop later, it is uncommon for BD and neuro-BD to be diagnosed at the same time. In such situations, comprehensive evaluation of both mucocutaneous signs and neurological manifestations—including cephalalgia, personality alterations, psychiatric disturbances, and localized neurological deficits—is crucial.

Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) constitutes a frequent etiology of hyponatraemia, often secondary to central nervous system (CNS) inflammation, neoplasms, or specific pharmacotherapies. Given the intense inflammatory activity associated with acute neuro-BD, these individuals may be predisposed to SIADH. Although there are accounts of SIADH developing in BD patients undergoing agents such as cyclophosphamide, evidence supporting SIADH arising exclusively from CNS inflammatory mechanisms—without medication-related triggers—in acute neuro-BD remains insufficiently documented.

We present an unusual instance in which BD and acute neuro-BD were simultaneously diagnosed, with the course complicated by SIADH. This case underscores the necessity of including SIADH in the differential diagnosis when evaluating patients with acute neuro-BD who exhibit hyponatraemia and vague CNS symptomatology.

Case presentation:

A man of Asian descent in his sixties arrived at the emergency department experiencing three days of fever, headache, general malaise, loss of appetite, ongoing hiccups, and joint pain localized to his shoulders and ankles. He also mentioned the presence of oral aphthous ulcers.

The patient’s medical background was notable for type 2 diabetes mellitus; however, he was not on medication due to limited awareness about his illness and insufficient attention to dietary and exercise recommendations. There was no significant family history, as none of his relatives had been diagnosed with BD. His social history revealed occasional alcohol consumption, no tobacco use, no recent travel, and no contact with animals or ill individuals. His occupation was in the metalworking sector, and he denied any recent sexual encounters.

Upon evaluation, the patient was conscious but showed signs of tiredness. His vital parameters were as follows: temperature measured at 38.1°C, blood pressure at 135/73 mm Hg, heart rate of 119 beats per minute with regular rhythm, respiratory rate at 19 breaths per minute, and oxygen saturation at 96% while breathing ambient air. On physical inspection, there were oral aphthous ulcers and folliculitis-like lesions located on the face and trunk (see figure 1A, B, and C). Assessment of the cardiopulmonary and abdominal systems was within normal limits. Manual testing of muscle strength in both upper and lower limbs demonstrated no evidence of weakness. There were no signs of peripheral edema, and clinical evaluation indicated a euvolemic state. The patient was hospitalized for additional assessment.

Investigations:

Upon admission, laboratory investigations demonstrated significant hyponatraemia (124 mmol/L), markedly increased creatine kinase (1349 U/L), as well as raised levels of aspartate aminotransferase (77 U/L), alanine aminotransferase (64 U/L), C-reactive protein (CRP) (180 mg/L; reference, <3 mg/L), and erythrocyte sedimentation rate (70 mm/hour; reference, <15 mm/hour), while renal function parameters remained within normal limits. Additionally, the patient exhibited hyperglycaemia (plasma glucose 14.2 mmol/L) and a high HbA1c (8.2%), reflecting inadequate glycaemic management. Screening for hepatitis B and C returned negative results.

Assessment of endocrine status indicated that thyroid-stimulating hormone (1.11 mIU/L; reference, 0.50–5.00 mIU/L), free T4 (1.25 ng/dL; reference, 0.90–1.70 ng/dL), and serum cortisol (42 µg/dL; reference, >6 µg/dL) were within normal reference ranges. Serum osmolality was reduced (267 mOsm/kg; reference, <270 mOsm/kg), whereas urine osmolality was substantially increased (900 mOsm/kg; reference, >300 mOsm/kg), accompanied by elevated urinary sodium (88 mmol/L; reference, >20 mmol/L), indicating compromised renal water clearance. Notably, despite the presence of hypo-osmolar hyponatraemia, antidiuretic hormone (ADH) concentration was increased (22.4 pg/mL). Electrocardiogram findings were unremarkable, as were imaging studies including chest X-ray and CT scans of the head, chest, and abdomen. Collectively, these results were consistent with a diagnosis of SIADH.

By the third day, the patient’s headache and vertigo had intensified. Despite the absence of nuchal rigidity or cranial nerve deficits, there was concern for central nervous system inflammation. Magnetic resonance imaging of the brain did not show any notable abnormalities affecting the hypothalamus or other regions (figure 2). Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) evaluation demonstrated a slightly elevated opening pressure (17 cmH₂O), increased protein content (0.97 g/L), and a modest lymphocytic pleocytosis (11 cells/µL). Microbiological studies of the CSF, including cultures and assays for bacterial and mycobacterial organisms, as well as HSV, VZV, and cryptococcal antigen, all yielded negative results. CSF cytology excluded the presence of malignant cells.

Additional diagnostic work-up was undertaken to identify possible underlying systemic illnesses. Markers for sarcoidosis, including lysozyme (5.1 µg/mL; reference 5–10) and angiotensin-converting enzyme (11.4 U/L; reference 7–25), were within normal limits. Hematological evaluation demonstrated an elevated soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) (704 U/mL; reference, 122–496 U/mL), but the patient tested negative for human T-cell leukaemia virus type 1 antibody.

By day 5, a painful ulceration developed on the scrotum (figure 1D). Alongside concurrent oral aphthae and rashes resembling folliculitis, this clinical picture was indicative of a systemic manifestation of BD. There was no evidence of hypersensitivity following a pinprick test. Ocular assessment did not detect any signs of uveitis. HLA-B51 testing was positive.

Differential diagnosis:

Upon admission, the constellation of symptoms—fever, headache, general malaise, and hyponatraemia—raised suspicion for either a central nervous system infection or a systemic inflammatory condition. Viral meningoencephalitis, especially from HSV or VZV, was initially considered likely. Nevertheless, cerebrospinal fluid analysis failed to detect viral antigens or antibodies, effectively ruling out these viral etiologies. Bacterial meningitis was also entertained as a differential diagnosis, but was excluded because of the absence of neutrophilic predominance, only slight lymphocytic pleocytosis, and negative CSF cultures. Tuberculous meningitis was similarly deemed unlikely, given negative results for acid-fast staining and tuberculosis DNA assays.

A primary CNS neoplasm was contemplated owing to the neurological presentation, but both the brain MRI and CSF cytology returned normal, providing no evidence for malignancy. Though a slight elevation in sIL-2R was detected, this laboratory finding was not sufficient by itself to confirm CNS lymphoma.

Autoimmune processes were also systematically assessed and excluded, as there were neither typical systemic manifestations nor positive findings on autoantibody screens. Neurosarcoidosis remained a remote possibility, given that MRI may initially appear normal in such cases; however, laboratory findings did not support this diagnosis, and the mildly increased sIL-2R level was ultimately interpreted as nonspecific.

The diagnosis of definite BD was based on the clinical picture, which featured fever, fatigue, oral aphthae (scoring two points), genital aphthosis (scoring two points), and a folliculitis-like rash (scoring one point), totaling five points as per the International Criteria for Behçet’s Disease (with a threshold of ≥4 points required). Genital aphthosis, in particular, is considered a highly specific manifestation of BD and lends strong support to this diagnosis. Following comprehensive exclusion of infectious, neoplastic, and autoimmune causes, the patient was diagnosed with acute neuro-BD, supported by the concurrence of CNS symptoms and mucocutaneous involvement.

Treatment:

Upon admission (day 1), clinicians suspected an underlying inflammatory disorder, though its precise origin was indeterminate. Empirical antimicrobial treatment with cefepime was commenced after blood cultures were obtained, addressing the possibility of a bacterial infection despite the lack of a clearly identifiable infectious site.

By day 3, the patient experienced increasing severity of headache and vertigo, raising concern for possible CNS infection or inflammation. Acyclovir therapy was empirically initiated but subsequently discontinued when laboratory testing excluded HSV and VZV encephalitis.

On day 8, colchicine therapy was introduced subsequent to diagnoses of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD. Management of oral aphthous ulcers involved topical corticosteroid ointment, while genital ulcers and cutaneous manifestations were addressed with silver sulfadiazine cream. SIADH was treated with conservative measures including restriction of fluid intake to less than 800 mL/day, close daily monitoring of serum sodium, and prevention of rapid sodium correction. The use of hypertonic saline and vasopressin receptor antagonists was not deemed necessary.

Outcome and follow-up:

The patient's fever subsided and his appetite increased the day following initiation of colchicine therapy. Over the next two weeks, his various symptoms—including vertigo, malaise, hiccups, and mucocutaneous lesions—showed gradual improvement. By the sixteenth day, his serum sodium had normalized at 136 mmol/L, and ADH concentrations had dropped to 1.8 pg/mL. Levels of myogenic enzymes also returned to within normal limits.

Notably, no systemic corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive agents were administered throughout his treatment course. The patient was discharged after one month in the hospital, during which the acute phase was resolved, hyponatraemia was vigilantly tracked and addressed, and rehabilitation was provided to address his physical deconditioning. Colchicine therapy was maintained for nearly four years without relapse of BD or recurrence of neurological manifestations. After stabilization of his BD, biguanide therapy was introduced, resulting in sustained good glycaemic control.

Discussion:

This case presented the uncommon concurrent diagnosis of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, underscoring a rare clinical scenario. Additionally, it showed that acute neuro-BD can be associated with SIADH. The resolution of mucocutaneous lesions, neurological symptoms, and hyponatraemia after colchicine monotherapy suggests there may be a direct relationship among BD, neuro-BD, and SIADH. In individuals with BD who develop fever or headache in conjunction with hyponatraemia, acute neuro-BD should be considered as a possible etiology.

A key point in this case was the concurrent identification of both systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, supported by mucocutaneous findings and central nervous system involvement. Major organ manifestations, such as CNS involvement, most often emerge approximately four years post-initial BD diagnosis, and neurological symptoms at first presentation are infrequent, appearing in less than 5% of cases. Direct headache attribution to neuro-BD is found in only about 10% of affected patients. Consequently, recognizing neuro-BD at disease onset was especially challenging in this instance. Acute neuro-BD typically manifests as a transient meningoencephalitis, which may or may not be accompanied by focal neurological signs. In this patient, brain MRI was unremarkable, failing to reveal changes in characteristic regions affected by BD, such as the hypothalamus. Acute neuro-BD can manifest as aseptic meningitis in the absence of brain parenchymal lesions, and a normal brain MRI—especially in early disease—does not eliminate the possibility of neuro-BD, complicating the diagnostic process further. Although HLA-B51 analysis was conducted, it did not aid diagnosis, consistent with international BD guidelines, which prioritize clinical findings and do not mandate HLA-B51. Ultimately, this case emphasizes the need to maintain a high index of suspicion for neuro-BD in BD patients with mucocutaneous symptoms who present with ambiguous neurological complaints.

At the outset of the differential diagnosis process, we initially focused on infectious etiologies for the CNS inflammation, prompted by the co-occurrence of unexplained fever, neurological manifestations, and hyponatraemia; nonetheless, no definitive infectious agent was detected. We subsequently broadened the differential to include additional inflammatory causes such as connective tissue disorders and malignancies, with specific attention to sarcoidosis and haematologic cancers. Consequently, a comprehensive initial assessment was undertaken, including assays for autoimmune serological markers, serum ACE, and sIL-2R, primarily to exclude possible diagnoses rather than as a response to strong clinical suspicion. These tests were thus ordered with a narrow intent as part of a strategy centered on ruling out certain conditions. Ultimately, there was no supporting evidence—clinical, radiological, or within CSF studies—affirming these potential diagnoses, and these ancillary tests did not materially assist in reaching a conclusive diagnosis. The diagnostic value of ACE for sarcoidosis remains limited due to its low sensitivity, and while sIL-2R can sometimes aid in the workup for malignant lymphoma, slight elevations lack specificity and generally indicate non-specific inflammatory activity. In this patient's scenario, the absence of genital ulceration early in the disease course complicated the consideration of BD, necessitating an expanded differential. This diagnostic trajectory underscores the necessity for judicious use of ancillary testing, reminding clinicians to reserve such investigations for situations with adequate clinical justification and to interpret findings with discernment.

While drug-induced SIADH has been documented in individuals with BD undergoing treatment with agents like cyclophosphamide, the link between acute neuro-BD and the development of SIADH has not been clearly elucidated. Neuro-BD involves inflammation within the central nervous system, a process that could potentially lead to abnormal ADH regulation. Prior research indicates that interleukin-6 (IL-6)—a proinflammatory cytokine commonly elevated in neuroinflammatory states—can drive increased ADH secretion. In the context of BD, monocyte dysregulation triggers spontaneous IL-6 production, and heightened concentrations of IL-6 have been observed in the cerebrospinal fluid of those experiencing acute neuro-BD. These observations imply that inflammation mediated by IL-6 may play a role in the pathogenesis of SIADH in this clinical scenario. Although this case report did not assess IL-6 levels in serum or CSF, the presence of a substantially increased serum CRP could serve as an indirect marker of an IL-6–related inflammatory process. Despite SIADH being linked to multiple conditions such as central nervous system inflammation, pulmonary disorders, neoplasms, and various pharmacological agents, the current evidence is insufficient to definitively establish causality between SIADH and acute neuro-BD. The sequence of events documented here may be indicative of a general response to CNS inflammation, rather than a mechanism unique to neuro-BD.

This case further demonstrates the range of complications that can arise from SIADH-induced hyponatraemia in acute neuro-BD. Prior literature has highlighted that persistent hiccups may provide a valuable clinical indication of severe hyponatraemia secondary to SIADH resulting from central nervous system inflammation, such as that seen in meningitis. Moreover, our assessment included measuring CK to investigate nonspecific symptoms such as fever, arthralgia, and generalized fatigue, given their potential link to inflammatory or metabolic muscle injury; this led to the detection of rhabdomyolysis. Although rhabdomyolysis is a well-documented, it is frequently neglected complication of marked hyponatraemia and is seldom specifically tested for. These clinical features, while recognized, are often insufficiently emphasized as significant sequelae of profound hyponatraemia. It is essential for clinicians to recognize that symptoms seemingly unrelated to electrolyte imbalance—including hiccups and rhabdomyolysis—may, in fact, signal hidden disturbances in electrolyte homeostasis, especially in the context of neuroinflammatory disease.

------
Corticosteroids remain the cornerstone therapy for acute neuro-BD, with the majority of patients exhibiting a positive therapeutic response. Nonetheless, there are documented instances of spontaneous improvement in patients whose clinical presentation is limited to meningoencephalitis-like features. In the case described here, administration of colchicine alone led to amelioration of both mucocutaneous and neurological manifestations, in addition to correcting hyponatraemia. Colchicine’s anti-inflammatory properties are mediated through the inhibition of neutrophil motility and activation, as well as the suppression of cytokine synthesis, particularly IL-1β and IL-6. Although colchicine is routinely prescribed to manage the mucocutaneous symptoms of BD, evidence from systematic reviews indicates that it does not have significant efficacy in treating acute neuro-BD, where immunosuppressants are typically necessary to achieve sufficient disease control. Indeed, the literature generally describes colchicine being used as an adjunctive agent alongside corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive medications, rather than as the sole therapy. While it is possible that the observed improvements in this case could be attributed to spontaneous recovery, these observations imply that colchicine might represent a viable therapeutic alternative for acute neuro-BD and associated complications such as SIADH, especially in scenarios where corticosteroid use is not feasible or is contraindicated.

Conclusion:

We reported an uncommon presentation of BD alongside simultaneous onset of acute neuro-BD, where SIADH emerged as a complication of neuro-BD. This case underscores that SIADH can arise secondary to acute neuro-BD, emphasizing the necessity for clinicians to recognize this possible link. Treatment with colchicine alone resulted in swift resolution of mucocutaneous lesions, neurological deficits, and hyponatraemia, indicating colchicine's potential as an effective early intervention for neuro-BD. For BD patients exhibiting vague central nervous system symptoms—such as fever and headache—accompanied by hyponatraemia, clinicians should remain vigilant for the possibility of acute neuro-BD complicated by SIADH.<|im_end|><|im_end|>.

It would be interesting to do some experimental and practical work, to assess the validity of the idea that malt can be made in a well, using a basket. I wondered how much malt could have been made in this way. There would not be enough to supply large vats like those at Hierakonopolis, a predynastic site in Egypt, which were probably used as mash tuns.

Beer was being made here in industrial quantities.

The vats, probably mash tuns, at Hierakonopolis, Egypt. Source here
The Egyptians must have used floors for germinating the barley. A malting floor is not an obvious thing to identify in the archaeological record. All that's required is a smooth level floor surface within a building. It's something that has been overlooked in the interpretation of archaeological sites. Archaeologists don't often interpret the remains of a building as having possibly been a grain barn or a floor surface as having been being suitable for making malt.

Eric talked about malting floors and the potential evidence for the manufacture of ale in prehistory. I was rather pleased that, on his next slide, he showed some of the evidence for the making of malt and ale in Neolithic Britain. That's my research.

There is good evidence for the manufacture of malt and ale at some of the Orcadian neolithic sites, such as Skara Brae and Barnhouse, six thousand years ago. At Skara Brae there were sherds from a large 30 gallon pot found beside the central hearth. Excavated by Vere Gordon Childe in the 1930s, no residue analysis was done on these pot sherds. Its' location would be an ideal spot for a fermenting barley wort. At the site of Barnhouse, another neolithic village, there was a possible malting floor as well as many drains which are essential for a brewer. Analysis of pot sherds using the technique of Gas Chromatography/Mass Spectrometry revealed "unidentified sugars and barley lipids" in the fabric of the pottery. It's a possible indication of sparging. If they were sparging to obtain a wort then they must have been making malt and ale. 

If you want to read more about neolithic and bronze age evidence, I suggest you have a look here at an article we wrote for Orkneyjar a few years ago. It's about the prehistoric transformation of grain into ale. You'll also find links to my published academic papers and to my thesis. I know of no other archaeologists who are working on the archaeological evidence for the manufacture of barley malt and ale in the neolithic. It seems to be considered a controversial topic by some academics, or so I am told. That's why I was pleased to see my work referred to at this malting seminar. Clearly the professional maltsters, brewers and distillers have no problem with the idea.

Eric described how floor malting is labour intensive, requiring skilled and dedicated people. It was the only way of making malt until the mid 19th century. The malt must be turned and raked on the floor, as well as being moved around the maltings from steep tank to germination floor, and from there to the kiln floor. It takes many workers to make the malt.

Modern pneumatic maltings changed all of this.

Two Belgian malting engineers, Galland and Saladin, are considered to be the fathers of modern pneumatic malting. In 1873 Galland designed a system of aerated rectangular boxes. A few years later, in 1880, Saladin introduced a mechanised turning system. The new integrated system of aeration and mechanical turning of the malt by Archimedes screws was called
a saladin box.

A few Saladin boxes are in use today, this one is at Tamdu Distillery
, source here

The workers who turned the malt cannot have been happy about this. For many, it meant the loss of their job. It was, in so many ways, a revolution in the malting industry, although it took several decades, until the 1960s, before Saladin boxes became commonplace in a maltings.

The technology of making malt developed rapidly. By the early 1970s, Saladin drums were being made and used. These are far larger and capable of producing thirty to fifty tonnes of malt at a time, in a continuous batching process. They can be operated all year round, in contrast to a traditional floor maltings that were subject to weather and temperature (see previous post). Much less manpower is needed, there is a lower cost of production. They consistently produce good quality malt in the quantity that is required today in the modern brewing and distilling business.

Germinating Kilning Vessel at Crisp's Maltings, source here

Germinating Kilning Vessels, can make up to 500 tons of malt at a time. I've not yet seen a large industrial maltings and have never looked inside one of these Germinating Kilning Vessels, but here's a man who has. Jeff Evans, on his blog inside beer, describes the experience:

"I climbed the stairs of one of these enormous, modern cylindrical towers to discover vast stainless-steel steeping tanks, malting floors and kilns. Having seen the simple, homely methods of the floor maltings, the scale and functionality came as a shock. Each of the tanks handles more than ten times the amount the entire floor malting facility can process in one go. 

At the push of a few buttons, the process gets underway – steeping, germination, kilning. Such are the efficiencies that germination is a day shorter here, and kilning only takes two days. The equipment is also much easier to clean, so you can see why new technology has its supporters in the industry. 

Once malted, the grains are quickly shipped out to breweries or malt merchants. Crisp’s job is done. The brewer will ultimately claim the credit for his wonderful beer, but, as the old saying puts it, you can’t make a silk purse out of a sow’s ear. There’s a lot of skill and care that goes into that drink even before the brewer starts his work. 

The next time you are entranced by a hoppy aroma as you raise a pint to your lips, stop, wait and think. Look beyond the green, tangy, zesty glory boys that steal the limelight and remember the malt and the maltster, the forgotten heroes of brewing."

In his final remarks about malt and malting techniques, Eric Walker reminded us of some of the fundamental principles.

Malt is a living thing. If it is not correctly steeped it can either drown or asphyxiate. It needs careful handling. The maltsters who make malt in these large vessels take as much care of it as the traditional floor maltster, there's a whole new set of skills and knowledge involved in the craft of making huge batches of good quality malt. The maltster's rub, whereby the maltster judges when it is ready for kilning, still applies. Ancient skills and knowledge are combined with modern technology.

Malting, he said

"is a process utilised by the ancients to produce palatable alcoholic beverages: ale and beer."

I couldn't agree more.

PS In one of my earlier blogs I've asked:  where have all the malting floors gone?

Post a comment<|endoftext|>Do Raccoons Frequent Your Property? Follow A Few Tips To Keep Them Away

Posted on: 7 October 2016


It may be quite enjoyable to spot wildlife in their natural habitat while on hiking or camping adventures. But, when you find an animal like a raccoon ravaging your plants or going through your trash, you might be thinking about what you can do to keep these bandits from causing trouble. A lot of what you can do revolves around prevention, whether it is adding or taking away certain things that they like or dislike. So, that is exactly what you should focus on to start seeing their numbers dwindle on your property.

Grow Deterrent Plants

Raccoons love to eat a lot of plants, but there are still quite a few that they are not fans of. Creating a treacherous territory to traverse is one way to get raccoons to think twice before entering your landscape. These plants include pumpkins, poppies, and thistle, all of which are common plants to have. It may require strategical placement to maximize their effectiveness, but a professional should be able to help you out, especially if you show them where the raccoons are causing the most problems.

Secure the Trash Cans

You can do tons of the work to your property's yard, but all it takes is an open trash can to continue giving them an invitation. It is crucial to always secure your trash can to fix this problem. One easy solution is to keep the bin inside your garage, but this can lead to an undesirable odor. Try putting a heavy object on the top or using a couple of bungee cords to secure the bin without having to tie any knots. Another option is making a spicy solution that deters the raccoons from getting nearby. A cayenne pepper mix is achievable by mixing a dash of dish soap, cayenne pepper, and a large amount of water in a spray bottle.

Skip the Bird Seed

It might be tempting to put out bird seed to invite some flying creatures to your property, but doing this also invites raccoons, squirrels, and even insects. It is best to transition into something less problematic such as a hummingbird feeder, in which the only thing you need to worry about is ants. Also, you do not need to provide hummingbirds with an open water source, as opposed to birds that benefit from a bath. So not giving raccoons a way to clean up and drink water will deter them from your yard.

These adjustments should be effective for controlling the raccoon population, but do not hesitate to take it a step further by hiring a pest control company like BugOut Pest Control to help you with guaranteed solutions.<|endoftext|>@conference {89259, title = {Does Production Worth and Breeding Worth reflect cow profitability?}, booktitle = {Proceedings of the New Zealand Society of Animal Production}, volume = {73}, year = {2013}, month = {Jan}, pages = {17-20}, publisher = {New Zealand Society of Animal Production}, organization = {New Zealand Society of Animal Production}, address = {Hamilton}, abstract = {

Production worth (PW) is an economic index estimating the efficiency that a cow converts feed into farm profit. Breeding worth (BW) is another economic index that estimates a cow{\textquoteright}s or sire{\textquoteright}s ability to breed profitable replacements. This study evaluates how well PW and BW are correlated with net income (NI) per 4.5 tonnes (t) of dry matter (DM) consumed on a pasture based New Zealand dairy farm. During the 2009/10 season at Massey Number 4 Dairy Farm, cow milk yield and composition were determined at monthly herd tests, and live weight data was automatically collected twice daily using a walk-over-weigh system. The results show that this herd is achieving a genetic gain of $6.74 BW per year. Correlations of 0.52 and 0.31 (P \<0.001) were found between NI per 4.5 t DM and PW and BW, respectively. The NI per 4.5 t DM was higher in cows between 3- to 9-years-old than first lactation cows. The NI per 4.5 t DM was also strongly correlated with the milk, fat and protein yields (P \<0.001). This study confirms that PW, rather than BW, better reflects the genetic capability of a cow to convert feed into farm profit.

Philosophy of Greens

No green tube paint will ever, or can ever, mimic the greens of the natural world.  I struggled for years, wondering which green most closely resembled nature.  And I came close when I said, 'chrome oxide green'; however, now I feel that no straight green from a tube is natural.  Mixed greens are more natural, more real to what we are capable of seeing with our eyes.  And greens are highly dependent upon their environments and surroundings in order to look correct or 'right'.  For this reason, green is a difficult color with which to paint.  Think of a painting that has attempted to use all green and has successfully pulled it off.  I cannot.  Perhaps this has become my new challenge: an attempt at green.  So much of woodland green is black or dark brown.  And so much of garden green is actually more focus on the colors of surrounding flowers.  Green is more than a mix of blue and is chlorophyll.  Is it possible to capture and paint the effects of chlorophyll?  Is that why green is so hard to paint with?  If it is, that means green is largely dependent on the sun for its physical properties.  So then, what does this mean for all the other colors?

-an excerpt from my journal 7/31/07

Friday, March 3, 2017

Memory: sitting & looking, thinking, reflecting, remembering.  It's a tricky thing sometimes.  It is part of us.  It helps make, create and, at times, define us.  I have a friend who swears by my memory and would not question it (as they say, 'an elephant never forgets'); though sometimes I do, based on memory jogs, internal brain misfirings and of course, self doubt.  Regardless, memory has always been strong with me, ingrained or perhaps burned into my brain.  At times it's hard to forget and others hard to let go.  There's both good memories and those that aren't so good, but all those were once experiences that helped shape who we are.  In growth, we take the good memories with the unpleasant ones.  I find sometimes that it's the unpleasant ones that at times are the most defining, leading to growth of character.  And as such, I love the memory of dreams; I don't mean the 'hopes and dreams' in life, but our actual dream state of sleep.  There is something even more peculiar about that type of memory, because many times a dream memory is not so lucid, but rather, tiny bits of information processing in a different functional state, conscious memory vs. sleep-dream sequence, or some filtering of both.  Like a definition, I find writing it all down, yet another function of memory, a written remembrance to look back upon from time to time; much like the art being created to feel and convey the physicality of memory.   

* the image is one of my 6"x6" encaustic paintings, combining layered papers, pigments and drawings: loose images I had saved in a sketchbook from 2004, text from a recent read, and a paper printout of memory that a peer had randomly handed me while teaching a class.  To see more in this series, please visity my website:<|endoftext|>Herding goats (and other animals)

A while back, I promised to tell a bit more about my work at the goat yard here at Ne’ot Semadar.

So this is what it looks like at the milking area of the Dir, which is the Hebrew word for goat yard.

The goats come into the milking area, stick their head into the trough, and activate a lock mechanism that prevents them from leaving until we’ve had time to milk them. So they’re stuck with their heads in the feed and their butts facing the world.

I get to look at a lot of goat butts these days, as I’m quite a few shifts milking and feeding the goats.

The Neot Semadar goat herd consists of 240 lactating female goats, 10 fully grown male goats, 60 small female goats and 5 small male goats.

The grown female goats are milked in the morning from around 5.45 and in the evening from around 16.30. Each goat produces around 2.5 litres of milk a day on average, so the daily total is around 600 litres.

All of that milk is taken to the local dairy to be turned into yoghurt, lassi, various kinds of cheeses and ice cream. Some is also just skimmed for use in the coffee or the kids’ cereal.

One of the best things about working with the goats is taking them out to pasture. Here the young female goats, the nigmalot, are grazing quietly around 7.30 am. And while they eat, you get to sit and contemplate, enjoying the morning as much as they do.

Some of the goats have more personality than others. And this one is especially outgoing. This is Nerchama (the ch is pronounced as in German like a throat clearing noise, not as the “ch” in “chair.”). It is a Hebrew name that means “consolation” or “comfort”.

Nerchama is one of the biggest, and thus one of the hungriest goats in the herd. This means that she will come back into the milking area when other goats are coming out to have seconds. On this photo she is in her favourite pose: Waiting for the gate to the milking area to open so that she can run in and snatch a few leftovers. 

She is quite strong, and usually the best tactic for getting her out of the milking area is to pull her by the ears, which, as you can see, are already quite long.

There are ten male goats that are kept separate from the rest of the herd, and for good reason. They only have one thing on their mind. Having seen male goats up close I understand fully why the devil is commonly depicted as half-goat half-man.

With their big horns, their long beards and their eyes with weird elongated pupils they look decidedly other-worldly up close. They are like the incarnation of the archetypical male features: They swagger, they butt horns and fight, they exude a heavy, sweet musk. In short, they are the incarnation of the desire to procreate. Some of them even come up and rub themselves against against us when we take them out to pasture.

As Isaac, who is one of the people in charge of the goat yard, says, the males are hot right now. And the time is close: Within a couple of weeks, they will start the mating.

Another interesting character of the goat yard is the goose. It follows the herd around everywhere. To the point where popular imagination has it that it is goose that thinks it is a goat. Here it is seen drinking from the goats’ water trough:

Story has it that there used to be two geese, and they would always make violent racket in the yard, annoying everybody who worked there. One day, one of the geese disappeared. Only a few days later was it discovered that it had – literally – fallen into a ditch and died. It was a hole dug for some irrigation piping, and it had been unable to climb up. The goat yard volunteers, rather cynically, rejoiced.

Yet another strange resident around the goat yard is this desert antilope. The kibbutzniks found it years ago lying injured close to the kibbutz, and they brought it in and took care of it. And it has been part of the herd ever since.

The goats mostly ignore it, and it just sort of does its own thing most of the time. But it is often around when there’s food to be had. Here it is joining in while the male goats are eating.

 One annoyance that comes with the territory in the Dir is the flies. The combination of warm goatshit and lots of fresh milk attracts a lot of flies. Apparently, they also like the warmth of the electrical kettle:

<|endoftext|>Paso Doble


The Paso Doble is a traditional, lively couple’s dance that despite its French origin was immediately adopted by the Spanish community. It is usually danced to music played at bullfights when the fighter enters the ring or just before a kill in a Spanish or Portuguese bullfight. Paso Doble mean ‘two step’. The dance became popular in the United States in the 1930s. In any dance sequence, the male is characterized as the bullfighter or matador and the lady as his cape. The dancers enact the role of the bull, picador, torero or Spanish dancer and can change roles during the routine.

Paso Doble is based on the Flamenco, and is considered more of a competitive dance form rather than a social dance. It is both arrogant and passionate, which are elements that must be portrayed by both partners. This style of dancing is a progressive dance form, and one of the most dramatic of Latin dances where dancers make strong movements while stepping forward with the heels. It also involves the integration of artistic hand movements all throughout the routine. The forward steps or walks are strong and proud. The male partner must incorporate a movement in which he stamps his foot like a matador striking the ground in order to attraction the bull’s attention. The movements are sharp and quick while the chest and head held high at all times to portray a mood of arrogance and dignity.

The Paso Doble is based on a 2/4 rhythm with a simple 1-2-1-2 march. There are very few rhythm changes throughout the routine. The temp is usually brisk ranging from 60 to 124 beats per minute. The basic rhythm is a march or one step per beat. One of the most interesting elements of the dance form is dramatic poses which both partners must perform in conjunction with the music. Some of the distinctive steps include Separation, Huit, Attack, Flamenco Taps and Spanish Line, to name a few.

Being synonymous with Spanish culture, the Paso Doble is undoubtedly one of the most exciting dances to learn especially if you want to be involved in competition or exhibition dancing. This is definitely not a dance for a crowded dance floor since it involves plenty of movement all around. It would be prudent to learn the Paso Doble along with a partner since you are not likely to encounter one at your local club so easily. Learning the dance form may be challenging but the rewards are plenty. Just make sure you are game for a great deal of acting and showmanship.

Should you have additional questions or want to signup for one of the dance classes, please call us at (212) 473-2623!<|endoftext|>Home About Us the Rabbis Contact Us

<|endoftext|>The Coke and Mentos Experiment


This 3 minutes movie was released about two weeks ago. It displays The Extreme Diet Coke & Mentos Experiment #137. Incredible and funny, is it not? I read somewhere that Mentos company hired both guys for their next advertising campaign.

Since we are in a serious place here, it was but underhand lead-in for a lesson of physics and chemistry: how can little Mentos have such a tremendous effect on a peaceful bottles of Diet Coke? Everyone will agree on the premises: the carbon dioxide (CO2) compressed into the soda escapes so rapidly that the pressure pushes it out of the bottle. It's like shaking a bottle before you open it, but even more dramatic.

Several people theorized that a substance called gum arabic in the Mentos breaks the surface tension of the soda, allowing the CO2 bubbles to escape rapidly. This explanation doesn't completely work though, since several items that contain no gum arabic also cause soda to foam violently.

The primary cause appears to be physical, not chemical: when a liquid is supersaturated with gas, like soda is with CO2, gas is able to form bubbles on nucleation sites (that are places with high surface area in a very small volume, such as scratches on a surface or specks of dust).

Mentos seem to be loaded with nucleation sites: there are so many microscopic nooks and crannies on their surface that an incredible number of bubbles will form when you drop one in a bottle of soda. Since the Mentos are also heavy enough to sink, they react with the soda all the way to the bottom. The escaping bubbles quickly turn into a raging foam, and the pressure builds dramatically.

This mechanism is somewhat similar to the way people whose urine is supersaturated with uric acid or calcium crystals often develop kidney stones. Fortunately enough, crystals are not gas, which probably explains why people with kidney stones usually don't explode.

\title{The Era of 1-bit LLMs: All Large Language Models are in 1.58 Bits}

\begin{abstract}
Building on advancements like BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, the landscape of 1-bit Large Language Models (LLMs) is rapidly evolving. In this study, we present a 1-bit LLM variation called \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, which represents all model parameters as ternary values chosen from \{-1, 0, 1\}. This architecture achieves parity with full-precision Transformer LLMs (using FP16 or BF16), maintaining similar levels of perplexity and performance across downstream tasks, while dramatically reducing costs in latency, memory usage, computational throughput, and energy demands. Significantly, the 1.58-bit LLM establishes a novel scaling law and training methodology that enables both efficient and high-performing future generations of LLMs. Moreover, this development introduces new computational paradigms and paves the way for specialized hardware tailored for 1-bit LLM workloads.
\end{abstract}

\section{The Era of 1-bit LLMs}

The proliferation of Large Language Models (LLMs) has led to considerable advances in artificial intelligence, particularly within natural language processing. Although these increasingly powerful models exhibit outstanding results across diverse tasks, their scale raises substantial obstacles for deployment and has intensified worries regarding the environmental footprint and financial cost associated with their significant energy demands.

A promising solution to these issues lies in post-training quantization, which seeks to compress model weights and activations by reducing their numerical precision~\cite{smoothquant, gptq, quip, quip_sharp}. This technique substantially decreases both the memory load and computation required for LLM inference, supporting a shift from 16-bit representations down to even 4-bit alternatives~\cite{gptq, awq}. Nevertheless, despite its widespread adoption in production LLMs, post-training quantization remains a sub-optimal strategy.

Recent advancements in 1-bit model architectures, exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, suggest a viable pathway for lowering the operational costs of LLMs without sacrificing accuracy. Standard LLMs typically utilize 16-bit floating-point representations (either FP16 or BF16), and their computations are largely dominated by matrix multiplications. Consequently, the primary computational expense arises from floating-point addition and multiplication. In contrast, BitNet's matrix multiplication exclusively utilizes integer addition, resulting in substantial reductions in energy consumption for LLMs. Given that power often constitutes the fundamental bottleneck in modern hardware accelerators, these energy savings may directly enhance computational throughput.

Beyond computation, the expense associated with shuttling model parameters from DRAM into the on-chip accelerator memory (such as SRAM) during inference is notable. Efforts to expand SRAM have aimed to improve throughput, but such expansions can incur costs far exceeding those of DRAM. 1-bit LLMs, in comparison to their full-precision counterparts, possess considerably smaller memory requirements in terms of both capacity and bandwidth. This property leads to reductions in both the financial and temporal costs of loading weights from DRAM, facilitating faster and more efficient inference.

Here, we present a novel 1-bit LLM extension named \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, characterized by ternary parameters that can assume values in \{-1, 0, 1\}. By introducing the zero value to the initial 1-bit BitNet design, BitNet b1.58 effectively encodes its parameters using 1.58 bits per entry in binary terms. This new variant preserves all key advantages of the original BitNet, including its fundamentally distinct computation framework that eliminates almost all multiplication during matrix multiplications and offers high optimization potential. BitNet b1.58 maintains equivalent energy efficiency and continues to excel in memory use, throughput, and latency relative to FP16 LLM benchmarks. In addition, BitNet b1.58 confers two further benefits: first, the integration of zero supports explicit feature filtering, which enhances its representational capacity and can lead to notable performance gains for 1-bit LLMs; second, experimental evidence demonstrates that, beginning at the 3B parameter scale and with equivalent configurations (such as model size and training tokens), BitNet b1.58 matches FP16 models in terms of perplexity and downstream task performance.

\section{BitNet b1.58}

\text{BitNet b1.58} builds upon the BitNet model, a Transformer variant in which the standard \emph{nn.Linear} layers are substituted by \emph{BitLinear} layers. Training is performed from the ground up, employing weights quantized to 1.58 bits and activations at 8 bits. While closely related to the initial BitNet, this version features several notable modifications, outlined below.

\paragraph{Quantization Function.} To limit the weights to values of -1, 0, or +1, we employ the \emph{absmean} quantization function. This approach initially rescales the weight matrix using the mean of its absolute values and subsequently maps each entry to the closest integer in \{-1, 0, +1\}:

\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{W} = \mathrm{RoundClip}(\frac{W}{\gamma + \epsilon}, -1, 1),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{RoundClip}(x, a, b) = \max(a, \min(b, \mathrm{round}(x))),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \gamma = \frac{1}{nm}\sum_{ij} |W_{ij}|.
\end{equation}

The activation quantization strategy remains consistent with the implementation in BitNet, aside from a key change: activations are not rescaled to the interval $[0, Q_b]$ prior to the application of non-linearities. Rather, they are uniformly normalized to $[-Q_b, Q_b]$ on a per-token basis, thereby eliminating zero-point quantization. This adjustment not only streamlines both coding and system optimization but, according to our experiments, incurs negligible impact on overall model performance.

\paragraph{LLaMA-alike Components.}

LLaMA~\cite{llama, llama2} serves as a predominant template for open-source LLM backbones. To better align with the open-source community, the architecture of \text{BitNet b1.58} incorporates components inspired by LLaMA. In particular, it utilizes RMSNorm~\cite{rmsnorm}, SwiGLU~\cite{swiglu}, and rotary embeddings~\cite{rope}, and excludes bias parameters throughout the network. This design choice ensures that \text{BitNet b1.58} can be easily plugged into widely used open-source toolkits, such as Huggingface, vLLM~\cite{vllm}, and llama.cpp\footnote{\url{https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp}}, requiring minimal adaptation.

\section{Results}

We benchmarked \text{BitNet b1.58} against our FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} replication across multiple model scales. For an equitable evaluation, both architectures underwent pre-training on the RedPajama dataset~\cite{redpajama} for 100 billion tokens. Their zero-shot capabilities were then tested across a diverse set of language understanding tasks: ARC-Easy~\cite{arc}, ARC-Challenge~\cite{arc}, Hellaswag~\cite{hellaswag}, Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, OpenbookQA~\cite{openbookqa}, and BoolQ~\cite{boolq}. In addition, we assessed validation perplexity using the WikiText2~\cite{wikitext2} and C4~\cite{c4} corpora.

The runtime GPU memory footprint and inference latency were evaluated for both \text{LLaMA LLM} and \text{BitNet b1.58}. All measurements were carried out using the FasterTransformer\footnote{\url{https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer}} framework, which provides efficient implementations for large-scale language model inference on GPUs. To further optimize \text{BitNet b1.58}, Ladder’s 2-bit kernel~\cite{ladder} was incorporated. We recorded the inference time per generated token, as this metric predominantly determines overall serving costs.

Perplexity and associated computational costs are provided in Table~\ref{tab:ppl} for both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. Notably, starting from the 3B parameter scale, \text{BitNet b1.58} approaches the perplexity achieved by the full-precision \text{LLaMA LLM}, while realizing 2.71x higher inference throughput and using only 28\% (i.e., 3.55x less) GPU memory. Specifically, with the 3.9B configuration, \text{BitNet b1.58} delivers 2.4x faster inference, requires 3.32x less memory, and also exceeds the performance of \text{LLaMA LLM} at the 3B scale.

Detailed breakdowns of zero-shot accuracy on downstream tasks can be found in Table~\ref{tab:zero-shot}. The assessment employed the \emph{lm-evaluation-harness}\footnote{\url{https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness}} methodology. These outcomes indicate a diminishing accuracy gap between \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} as model sizes scale up. Of particular significance, from the 3B size onward, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves parity with the full-precision reference. Mirroring perplexity trends, \text{BitNet b1.58} with 3.9B parameters outperforms \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B in both task accuracy and resource efficiency, underscoring its Pareto superiority over leading LLM systems.

\paragraph{Memory and Latency}

We expanded our experiments to include models of 7B, 13B, and 70B parameters and systematically assessed the associated computational cost. Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} presents the patterns observed in both latency and memory usage. Notably, the speed-up effect is more pronounced with increased model scale. Specifically, \text{BitNet b1.58} at 70B parameters demonstrates a 4.1-fold reduction in inference time relative to the \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline. This improvement can be attributed to the fact that the computational burden of \emph{nn.Linear} layers grows proportionally with model size. A parallel pattern appears in memory usage: since the embedding layer remains at full precision, its contribution to total memory consumption decreases as the overall model becomes larger. The reported values for both latency and memory are based on inference with a 2-bit kernel, leaving additional potential for future cost optimizations.

\paragraph{Energy}

Our analysis also includes an estimation of the energy required for arithmetic operations in both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. The evaluation focuses predominantly on matrix multiplication, which is the primary driver of computational cost in LLMs. As illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:energy}, most of the energy for \text{BitNet b1.58} comes from INT8 addition, whereas \text{LLaMA LLM} consumes energy through both FP16 addition and multiplication. Based on the energy consumption model outlined in~\cite{energycost, pokebnn}, matrix multiplications on 7nm process nodes using \text{BitNet b1.58} achieve a 71.4-fold reduction in arithmetic operations energy compared to \text{LLaMA LLM}. Furthermore, we calculated end-to-end energy consumption for processing 512 tokens, demonstrating that \text{BitNet b1.58} grows even more energy efficient relative to the FP16-based \text{LLaMA LLM} as model size increases. This trend arises because the relative computational load of \emph{nn.Linear} grows with model scale, whereas other components contribute less to the overall cost in larger architectures.

\paragraph{Throughput}

To assess throughput, we conducted a comparison between \text{BitNet b1.58} and the \text{LLaMA LLM} model with 70B parameters, deploying both on two 80GB A100 GPUs. Pipeline parallelism~\cite{gpipe} was utilized to accommodate the 70B \text{LLaMA LLM} across the devices. The batch size was progressively increased up to the available GPU memory limit, maintaining a sequence length of 512 throughout the evaluation. According to Table~\ref{tab:throughput}, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B is capable of supporting batch sizes that are up to eleven times larger than those manageable by \text{LLaMA LLM}. This translates into throughput that is 8.9 times higher for \text{BitNet b1.58}.

\textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58} introduces a novel scaling law connecting model efficiency and inference cost.} Drawing from the data in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} and Figure~\ref{fig:energy}, an approximate correspondence between models operating in 1.58-bit and 16-bit precision can be established as follows:

\begin{itemize}
    \item The 13B BitNet b1.58 outperforms the 3B FP16 LLM regarding latency, memory footprint, and energy efficiency.
    \item The 30B BitNet b1.58 surpasses the 7B FP16 LLM on the same three efficiency measures.
    \item The 70B BitNet b1.58 exceeds the 13B FP16 LLM in latency, memory use, and energy consumption.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Training with 2T Tokens}

Token count is a key determinant in the development of LLMs. To evaluate token-level scaling for \text{BitNet b1.58}, we trained this model on 2T tokens, following the training data methodology employed for StableLM-3B~\cite{StableLM-3B-4E1T}, the leading open-source 3B model. Both \text{BitNet b1.58} and StableLM-3B were assessed using a benchmark suite comprised of Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, SciQ~\cite{sciq}, LAMBADA~\cite{lambada}, and ARC-easy~\cite{arc}. Table~\ref{tab:2t} presents the reported zero-shot accuracy; for tasks quantified by accuracy and normalized accuracy, their mean is used. The reported performance of StableLM 3B at 2T tokens is sourced directly from its original technical report. Our evaluation demonstrates that \text{BitNet b1.58} delivers consistently higher results across all benchmark tasks, providing evidence that 1.58-bit LLMs exhibit strong generalization as well.

\section{Discussion and Future Work}

\textbf{1-bit Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs}

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have emerged as an efficient and scalable option for large language models, primarily by reducing the overall computational FLOPs required. However, their practical scalability has been constrained by high memory demands and the overhead incurred through inter-device communication. Adopting 1.58-bit LLMs has the potential to alleviate these issues. By lowering the memory footprint, fewer hardware units are necessary to host MoE models. In addition, the reduced size of activations helps minimize network communication costs during activation transfer. Ideally, this could enable entire models to reside on a single processing chip, thereby removing such overheads altogether.

\textbf{Native Support of Long Sequence in LLMs}

Efficient handling of long sequences has become increasingly essential for modern LLM applications. A persistent obstacle in long-sequence inference is the extensive memory consumption related to key-value (KV) caches. \text{BitNet b1.58} marks a significant advance by compressing activations from 16 bits to 8 bits, effectively doubling the maximum sequence length manageable with fixed resources. For 1.58-bit LLMs, activations could be compressed even further—to 4 bits or less—without loss of information, a direction we plan to investigate in future research.

\textbf{LLMs on Edge and Mobile}

Deploying 1.58-bit LLMs offers significant improvements for running language models on edge and mobile hardware, where constraints in both memory and processing power often limit model size and effectiveness. Due to their substantially decreased memory and energy needs, 1.58-bit LLMs can be more readily deployed on such resource-constrained platforms, unlocking a host of applications that previously would have been impractical. This paves the way for more sophisticated uses of LLMs in portable contexts. Additionally, since 1.58-bit LLMs are more compatible with CPUs—which dominate edge and mobile systems—\text{BitNet b1.58} can be executed efficiently on these platforms, offering further performance and capability gains.

\textbf{New Hardware for 1-bit LLMs}

Emerging efforts, such as those exemplified by Groq\footnote{\url{https://groq.com/}}, illustrate the feasibility and advantages of specialized hardware (e.g., LPUs) optimized for LLM workloads. Building on these developments, we propose and encourage the design of dedicated hardware and systems tailored specifically to the needs and computational properties of 1-bit LLMs, in light of the innovative computational paradigm established by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}.<|im_end|>

Acute neurological Behçet’s disease complicated by a syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone

Eiichi Kakehi, Yukako Matsumoto, Shigehisa Sakurai, Kazuhiko Kotani

Abstract:

Summary: This report details an uncommon presentation of acute neurological Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD) occurring simultaneously with systemic Behçet’s disease (BD)—classified as definite BD—complicated further by the onset of syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). A male patient in his 60s arrived with symptoms of fever and headache, alongside mucocutaneous signs such as oral aphthae, folliculitis-like eruptions, genital ulcers, and evidence of hyponatraemia. Diagnosis of BD was established on the basis of mucocutaneous findings. Although brain MRI did not reveal abnormalities, cerebrospinal fluid analysis identified aseptic meningitis, providing support for a diagnosis of acute neuro-BD. Additional laboratory testing satisfied the diagnostic criteria for SIADH. Treatment with colchicine alone was effective in resolving the patient’s cutaneous, neurological, and electrolyte abnormalities, obviating the need for corticosteroids or further immunosuppressive therapies. This case highlights a potential link between acute neuro-BD and SIADH, and emphasizes the clinical significance of identifying both neurological and endocrine involvement at the initial presentation of BD.

Background:

Behçet’s disease (BD) is a persistent, systemic inflammatory condition that is marked by episodic oral and genital ulcerations, uveitis, and dermatological manifestations. Neurological manifestations, collectively termed neuro-Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD), affect around 10%–20% of individuals with BD, often emerging years after the disease is initially recognized. The acute phase of neuro-BD frequently manifests as meningoencephalitis with parenchymal lesions, particularly impacting the brainstem. While it is uncommon for BD and neuro-BD to be identified simultaneously, such cases do occur. When confronted with concurrent diagnoses, healthcare providers must thoroughly evaluate both the mucocutaneous presentation and neurological findings, which can include headache, behavioral disturbances, psychiatric manifestations, and localized neurological impairments.

The syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) represents a frequent etiology of hyponatraemia, commonly arising in the setting of central nervous system (CNS) inflammation, malignancy, or as an adverse effect of specific pharmacotherapies. In light of the proinflammatory milieu associated with acute neuro-BD, patients with this condition could potentially develop SIADH. Although there are documented cases of SIADH in BD patients undergoing cyclophosphamide therapy, the incidence of SIADH directly attributable to CNS inflammation from acute neuro-BD, in the absence of medication triggers, is not well established.

Here, we describe an unusual case involving simultaneous diagnoses of BD and acute neuro-BD, further complicated by the development of SIADH. This case underscores the necessity of considering SIADH in the differential diagnosis of acute neuro-BD, especially when evaluating patients with ambiguous CNS complaints and hyponatraemia.

Case presentation:

A man of Asian descent in his sixth decade arrived at the emergency department, reporting three days of fever, headache, general fatigue, diminished appetite, continuous hiccups, and joint pain in the shoulders and ankles. He further mentioned experiencing oral aphthous ulcers.

His past medical history was significant for type 2 diabetes mellitus; however, he was not on any therapeutic regimen, largely due to insufficient understanding of his diagnosis and a lack of adherence to dietary and exercise recommendations. There was no notable family history of BD. The patient drank alcohol occasionally, did not smoke, had neither recently traveled nor had contact with animals or sick individuals. Employed in the metalworking sector, he reported no recent sexual encounters.

Upon evaluation, the patient was conscious but showed signs of tiredness. Recorded vital parameters included a body temperature of 38.1°C, blood pressure measuring 135/73 mm Hg, regular heart rate at 119 beats per minute, respiratory rate at 19 breaths per minute, and oxygen saturation of 96% while breathing ambient air. Physical assessment identified oral aphthous ulcers and folliculitis-like eruptions on the facial and truncal regions (figure 1A, B and C). Cardiopulmonary and abdominal assessments yielded normal results. Manual muscle strength testing demonstrated intact strength in both upper and lower limbs. There was an absence of peripheral edema, and his clinical volume assessment indicated euvolemia. Consequently, he was hospitalized for further diagnostic workup.

Investigations:

Initial laboratory investigations showed significant hyponatraemia (124 mmol/L), alongside increased levels of creatine kinase (1349 U/L), aspartate aminotransferase (77 U/L), alanine aminotransferase (64 U/L), C-reactive protein (CRP) (180 mg/L; reference range, <3 mg/L), and erythrocyte sedimentation rate (70 mm/hour; reference range, <15 mm/hour), while renal function remained within normal limits. The patient exhibited hyperglycaemia (plasma glucose 14.2 mmol/L) and a raised HbA1c of 8.2%, suggestive of suboptimal glycaemic control. Serological tests for hepatitis B and C were negative.

Endocrine assessment demonstrated normal values for thyroid-stimulating hormone (1.11 mIU/L; reference, 0.50–5.00 mIU/L), free T4 (1.25 ng/dL; reference, 0.90–1.70 ng/dL), and serum cortisol (42 µg/dL; reference, >6 µg/dL). Serum osmolality was reduced (267 mOsm/kg; reference, <270 mOsm/kg), while urine osmolality was markedly elevated (900 mOsm/kg; reference, >300 mOsm/kg), accompanied by a high urinary sodium level (88 mmol/L; reference, >20 mmol/L), indicating a disturbance in water excretion. Despite the presence of hypo-osmolar hyponatraemia, antidiuretic hormone (ADH) was found to be elevated (22.4 pg/mL). Electrocardiogram results were normal, and imaging studies, including chest X-ray and computed tomography of the head, chest, and abdomen, were unremarkable. Collectively, these findings were consistent with a diagnosis of SIADH.

By the third day, the patient’s headache and vertigo had intensified. Despite the absence of neck rigidity or evidence of cranial nerve dysfunction, central nervous system (CNS) inflammation was considered as a possible diagnosis. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) of the brain did not demonstrate any noteworthy abnormalities in the hypothalamus or other brain areas (figure 2). Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) studies showed a mildly increased opening pressure (17 cmH₂O), elevated protein concentration (0.97 g/L), and a slight lymphocytic pleocytosis (11 cells/µL). Microbiological tests on the CSF—including cultures and specific assays for bacterial and mycobacterial organisms, herpes simplex virus (HSV), varicella zoster virus (VZV), and cryptococcal antigen—were negative. CSF cytological analysis detected no evidence of malignant cells.

Additional diagnostic workup was undertaken to rule out underlying systemic pathologies. Evaluation for sarcoidosis demonstrated normal values for lysozyme (5.1 µg/mL; reference 5–10) and angiotensin-converting enzyme (11.4 U/L; reference 7–25). Screening for hematologic malignancy showed an increased level of soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) (704 U/mL; reference 122–496 U/mL), whereas testing for human T-cell leukemia virus type 1 antibody was negative.

On the fifth day, the patient developed a painful ulcer on the scrotum (figure 1D). Taken together with the presence of oral aphthae and folliculitis-like skin lesions, these manifestations raised the suspicion of systemic BD. There was no evidence of pathergy on skin testing. Examination by ophthalmology excluded the presence of uveitis. Testing for HLA-B51 was positive.

Differential diagnosis:

Upon initial assessment, the concurrence of fever, headache, general malaise, and hyponatraemia indicated a potential central nervous system (CNS) infection or a systemic inflammatory condition. Viral meningoencephalitis, especially due to HSV or VZV, was suspected first; nevertheless, cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis was negative for viral antigens and antibodies, thereby ruling out these viral etiologies. Consideration was also given to bacterial meningitis, but this diagnosis was discounted due to the absence of neutrophilic predominance, the presence of only mild lymphocytic pleocytosis, and negative CSF cultures. Tuberculous meningitis was similarly excluded based on negative acid-fast staining and tuberculosis DNA assays.

Given the neurologic symptoms, a primary CNS malignancy was also contemplated. However, both brain MRI and CSF cytology did not reveal any pathological abnormalities or malignant cells. Although the serum sIL-2R was slightly increased, this isolated result was not sufficient to diagnose CNS lymphoma.

The possibility of autoimmune disorders was explored; these were dismissed in light of the lack of hallmark systemic features and negative results on screening autoantibody tests. Neurosarcoidosis remained in the differential, considering that MRI may appear normal early on, yet subsequent laboratory findings argued against this diagnosis. The marginally elevated sIL-2R was interpreted as non-specific.

A definitive diagnosis of BD was made according to clinical manifestations, including fever and fatigue, oral aphthae (two points), genital aphthosis (two points), and a rash resembling folliculitis (one point). Utilizing the International Criteria for Behçet’s Disease, the patient accumulated a total of five points (≥4 signifying a confirmed diagnosis). Notably, genital aphthosis is recognized as a highly specific feature of BD, further substantiating this diagnosis. Following systematic exclusion of infectious, neoplastic, and autoimmune etiologies, acute neuro-BD was diagnosed on the basis of CNS symptoms in association with characteristic mucocutaneous findings.

Treatment:

Upon admission (day 1), clinicians suspected an underlying inflammatory disorder, although its precise cause was not immediately identified. Empiric cefepime therapy was commenced following the collection of blood cultures, aimed at addressing a possible bacterial infection even though no clear infectious site was evident.

By day 3, the patient's headache and vertigo had intensified, raising concerns for central nervous system inflammation or infection. As a precaution, acyclovir was introduced, but subsequently discontinued when diagnostic evaluations excluded HSV and VZV encephalitis.

On day 8, following the confirmation of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, colchicine treatment was initiated. Management of oral aphthous ulcers involved the application of topical corticosteroid ointment, while genital and skin lesions were treated with silver sulfadiazine cream. The syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) was approached with conservative measures, including fluid intake restriction (<800 mL/day), daily serum sodium assessments, and careful avoidance of rapid sodium correction. There was no necessity for hypertonic saline administration or the use of vasopressin receptor antagonists.

Outcome and follow-up:

The patient experienced resolution of fever and improvement in appetite the day following the initiation of colchicine therapy. Over the next 2 weeks, symptoms such as vertigo, general malaise, hiccups, and mucocutaneous lesions showed progressive amelioration. By the 16th day, his serum sodium concentration had returned to a normal value of 136 mmol/L, and ADH levels had declined to 1.8 pg/mL. Myogenic enzyme concentrations also normalized.

Notably, systemic corticosteroids and additional immunosuppressive agents were not administered throughout the course of treatment. The patient was discharged after a hospitalization period of 1 month, during which the acute phase was resolved, hyponatraemia was closely observed and addressed, and rehabilitation addressed declines in physical capability. Colchicine treatment was sustained for about 4 years, with no relapses of BD or neurological symptoms recorded during that period. Once BD was stabilized, biguanide therapy commenced, resulting in maintained satisfactory glycaemic control.

Discussion:

In this instance, systemic BD and acute neuro-BD were identified concurrently, a particularly uncommon clinical occurrence. The case further illustrated that acute neuro-BD may be accompanied by SIADH. The alleviation of mucocutaneous symptoms, neurological deficits, and hyponatraemia following colchicine monotherapy indicates a likely direct relationship among BD, neuro-BD, and SIADH. Thus, clinicians should entertain the possibility of acute neuro-BD in BD patients who exhibit fever or headache in association with hyponatraemia.

This report is significant for documenting the concurrent onset of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, evidenced by both mucocutaneous involvement and central nervous system manifestations. Conventionally, serious organ involvement such as CNS pathology emerges roughly four years after the initial BD diagnosis, with neurological symptoms at initial presentation observed in less than 5% of cases. Additionally, only about 10% of individuals experience headaches attributable directly to neuro-BD, making early identification especially difficult in this scenario. Acute neuro-BD most frequently presents as a self-limited meningoencephalitic process, occasionally accompanied by focal neurological impairments. In this patient, MRI did not disclose pathognomonic changes in standard BD-associated regions, such as the hypothalamus. Acute neuro-BD may manifest as aseptic meningitis without visible parenchymal abnormalities, and unremarkable brain imaging—especially early in the clinical course—cannot reliably exclude this diagnosis. The absence of definitive radiological features added further diagnostic complexity. Testing for HLA-B51 was conducted but did not assist in confirmation, reflecting the international diagnostic standards for BD, which prioritize clinical presentation over genetic testing. Overall, this case underscores the need to consider neuro-BD in individuals with mucocutaneous disease who develop vague neurological symptoms.

During the early stages of forming the differential diagnosis, we gave primary consideration to infection as the source of CNS inflammation, prompted by the concurrence of unexplained fever, neurological manifestations, and hyponatraemia. Nonetheless, our investigations did not detect any particular infectious agent. Alternative etiologies for CNS inflammation were also explored, such as connective tissue disorders and neoplastic conditions, including sarcoidosis and haematological malignancies. Consequently, we conducted an extensive initial screening by measuring autoimmune serological markers, serum ACE, and sIL-2R. These laboratory assessments were implemented selectively, as part of an exclusionary process rather than out of high clinical suspicion. Ultimately, neither clinical evaluation, imaging, nor CSF analysis yielded findings to corroborate these conditions, and these ancillary investigations offered little toward establishing the diagnosis. Given the limited sensitivity of ACE for sarcoidosis, and considering that while sIL-2R can be helpful in suspected malignant lymphoma, its mild elevation is not specific and may simply indicate general inflammatory activity. Notably, the lack of genital ulcers in the early presentation complicated the identification of BD, necessitating a comprehensive differential. This experience underscores the necessity for judicious use of diagnostic testing and reiterates that investigations should be reserved for cases with appropriate clinical indications, with results interpreted prudently.

While instances of drug-induced SIADH have been described in individuals with BD who are treated with agents such as cyclophosphamide, the connection between acute neuro-BD and SIADH remains inadequately elucidated. Neuro-BD involves inflammation within the CNS, a process that could potentially lead to abnormal regulation of ADH secretion. Previous research has demonstrated that interleukin-6 (IL-6), a proinflammatory cytokine commonly elevated in neuroinflammatory conditions, is linked to increased ADH secretion. In the context of BD, dysregulated monocyte activity results in spontaneous production of IL-6, and heightened IL-6 concentrations have been observed in the CSF of those experiencing acute neuro-BD episodes. Collectively, these observations imply that IL-6–driven inflammation may play a role in the development of SIADH in this context. Although IL-6 was not quantified in either serum or CSF for the current case, the significantly increased serum CRP may serve as an indirect indicator of an IL-6–dependent inflammatory process. Despite SIADH being known to arise in association with multiple conditions—including CNS inflammation, pulmonary disease, neoplasia, and use of certain drugs—the evidence from this case does not establish a direct causative association between SIADH and acute neuro-BD. The temporal relationship observed here might represent a general inflammatory reaction within the CNS rather than a mechanism specific to neuro-BD.

This case further highlights the risks associated with hyponatraemia due to SIADH in patients with acute neuro-BD. Earlier literature has indicated that persistent hiccups can act as a significant clinical indicator of severe hyponatraemia arising from SIADH secondary to central nervous system inflammation, such as in cases of meningitis. Additionally, during our initial assessment for fever, arthralgia, and generalized fatigue—which could signify either inflammatory or metabolic muscle injury—we evaluated CK levels. This assessment revealed rhabdomyolysis, a recognized yet frequently neglected complication of severe hyponatraemia that is not commonly screened for. Although these clinical features are documented consequences of marked hyponatraemia, they often go underrecognized in practice. It is essential for clinicians to recognize that symptoms appearing to be unrelated, like hiccups or rhabdomyolysis, might indicate significant electrolyte imbalances, especially amidst neuroinflammatory disorders.
